--- tbl_exhibits.description id=1
  BEFORE: "<div class=\"description-body\">\n    <p>In 1933, the year of Hitler's rise to power, approximately 160,000 Jews lived in Berlin, Germany, which was less than 4% of its population. By 1939, an estimated 80,000 Berlin Jews had managed to emigrate. Between 1941 and 1944 more than 60,000 were deported to Eastern European ghettoes and death camps. Only about 7,000 were known to have survived by 1945.</p>\n    <p>The historic documents in the Lowenstein Family Papers and Art collection tell the story of one Jewish family's miraculous survival amidst the horrors of the Holocaust. Two exceedingly rare documents from 1942 served as eviction notices. They order the recipients to report at a certain date and time to a government building in Berlin. In reality the notice was a summons of deportation to death camps. If obeyed, the recipient was killed. If not obeyed, the recipient most certainly did not retain the letter. That notice led to the deaths of an estimated 60,000 Jews.</p>\n    <p>The Loewensteins (Lowenstein after immigration) - Max, Maria, Karin, and Henry - lived in Berlin and, beginning in 1933, experienced the ever tightening Nazi noose. Like many other Jewish families they tried desperately to find ways to leave Germany. The beginnings of the Holocaust burst forth on Kristallnacht in 1938. Synagogues were burned and thousands of Jews were taken to concentration camps. Many were never seen again.</p>\n    <p>Thirteen-year-old Henry was lucky to be one of 10,000 children to be saved by the Kindertransport in 1939. Kindertransports were organized by British aid organizations to bring predominantly German, Austrian, and Czechoslovakian Jewish children to the United Kingdom. Henry was able to reunite with his family in 1947.</p>\n    <p>Henry's mother Maria, born into a Lutheran family, used her Aryan status to protect her loved ones. Her courage saved the family from deportation and certain death on numerous occasions. She brought the documents in this collection to America in 1946.</p>\n    <p>Henry Lowenstein donated them to the Ira M. and Peryle Hayutin Beck Memorial Archives. The collection is located in the Anderson Academic Commons, Special Collections and Archives.</p>\n    <div class=\"item-1-blocks\" style=\"width: 100%; margin-top: 50px; text-align: center; font-size: 19px\">\n    \n    <div class=\"container\">\n    <div class=\"row\">\n    <div class=\"col-lg-6\" style=\"margin-bottom: 30px\">\n    <div style=\"background: rgb(165,87,41); padding: 30px; border-radius: 25px; margin-right: 15px; margin-left: 15px;\" class=\"row\">\n    <div class=\"col-12\">\n    <p>More Information available at:</p>\n    <div class=\"row\">\n    <div class=\"col-md-6\">\n        <div style=\"display: table; height: 100%; width: 100%\">\n            <div style=\"display: table-cell; vertical-align: bottom\">\n                <p>Loewenstein Family Papers and Art Finding aid</p>\n                <a href=\"https://libguides.du.edu/c.php?g=629200&amp;p=4391853\" type=\"button\" style=\"background-color: rgb(64,21,15); border: none; color: #fff; border-radius: 25px; padding: 8px 20px; text-transform: uppercase;\">finding aid</a>\n            </div>\n        </div>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"col-md-6\">\n        <div style=\"display: table; height: 100%; width: 100%\">\n            <div style=\"display: table-cell; vertical-align: bottom\">\n                <p>View the Entire Collection</p>\n                <a href=\"https://specialcollections.du.edu/object/9eded850-fb8e-4e39-bf9c-ea26dd7893d4\" type=\"button\" style=\"background-color: rgb(64,21,15); border: none; color: #fff; border-radius: 25px; padding: 8px 20px; text-transform: uppercase;\">collection</a>\n            </div>\n        </div>\n    </div>\n</div>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    <div class=\"col-lg-6\">\n    <div class=\"row\" style=\"background: rgb(140, 64, 50); padding: 30px; border-radius: 25px; margin-right: 15px; margin-left: 15px;\">\n    <div class=\"col-12\">\n    <p>Please complete this feedback form for a 1-minute survey to helps us improve and engage our exhibits with different audiences.</p>\n    <a href=\"https://udenver.qualtrics.com/jfe/form/SV_9uc3OOeJuJLMM3Y\" type=\"button\" style=\"background-color: rgb(64,21,15); border: none; color: #fff; border-radius: 25px; padding: 8px 20px; text-transform: uppercase;\">survey</a>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    </div>\n    </div>"
  AFTER:  "<p>In 1933, the year of Hitler's rise to power, approximately 160,000 Jews lived in Berlin, Germany, which was less than 4% of its population. By 1939, an estimated 80,000 Berlin Jews had managed to emigrate. Between 1941 and 1944 more than 60,000 were deported to Eastern European ghettoes and death camps. Only about 7,000 were known to have survived by 1945.</p>\n    <p>The historic documents in the Lowenstein Family Papers and Art collection tell the story of one Jewish family's miraculous survival amidst the horrors of the Holocaust. In reality the notice was a summons of deportation to death camps. If obeyed, the recipient was killed. If not obeyed, the recipient most certainly did not retain the letter. That notice led to the deaths of an estimated 60,000 Jews.</p>\n    <p>The Loewensteins (Lowenstein after immigration) - Max, Maria, Karin, and Henry - lived in Berlin and, beginning in 1933, experienced the ever tightening Nazi noose. Like many other Jewish families they tried desperately to find ways to leave Germany. The beginnings of the Holocaust burst forth on Kristallnacht in 1938. Synagogues were burned and thousands of Jews were taken to concentration camps. Many were never seen again.</p>\n    <p>Thirteen-year-old Henry was lucky to be one of 10,000 children to be saved by the Kindertransport in 1939. Kindertransports were organized by British aid organizations to bring predominantly German, Austrian, and Czechoslovakian Jewish children to the United Kingdom. Henry was able to reunite with his family in 1947.</p>\n    <p>Henry's mother Maria, born into a Lutheran family, used her Aryan status to protect her loved ones. Her courage saved the family from deportation and certain death on numerous occasions. --- tbl_exhibits.description id=2
  BEFORE: "HYLAEA was an interactive site-specific installation created by artist Tim Weaver for the library at the University of Denver. Weaver, an associate professor and director of the Emergent Digital Practices program in the School of Art and Art History, combined images, objects, sounds, and videos to reanimate extinct species to awaken memories of lost life forms. This exhibition was on display at University Libraries (formerly Penrose Library) from October 14, 2010 through February 14, 2011.\nThe exhibit’s intent is to bring together separate artifacts of lost ecological memory. These artifacts are the deep colors and textures of extinct birds, sonic translations of the DNA and proteins of missing species, and books containing the published records of North America’s breathtaking wildlife. These sounds and images of lost habitats create an immersive sensory experience for the audience. Mining the University Libraries' Special Collections and Archives and the extinct species cabinets at the Denver Museum of Nature and Science, Weaver's exploration of artifacts and lost ecological memory brings records of human-induced extinctions to life."
  AFTER:  "<p>HYLAEA was an interactive site-specific installation created by artist Tim Weaver for the library at the University of Denver. Weaver, an associate professor and director of the Emergent Digital Practices program in the School of Art and Art History, combined images, objects, sounds, and videos to reanimate extinct species to awaken memories of lost life forms. This exhibition was on display at University Libraries (formerly Penrose Library) from October 14, 2010 through February 14, 2011.<br>The exhibit’s intent is to bring together separate artifacts of lost ecological memory. These artifacts are the deep colors and textures of extinct birds, sonic translations of the DNA and proteins of missing species, and books containing the published records of North America’s breathtaking wildlife. These sounds and images of lost habitats create an immersive sensory experience for the audience. Mining the University Libraries' Special Collections and Archives and the extinct species cabinets at the Denver Museum of Nature and Science, Weaver's exploration of artifacts and lost ecological memory brings records of human-induced extinctions to life.</p>"
--- tbl_exhibits.description id=3
  BEFORE: "<div><div><p>Founded in 1864, the University of Denver (DU) has a history of athletic team nicknames, mascots, and university branding that draw heavily on the identity as the oldest private university in Colorado. The history of DU is also forever connected to the Sand Creek Massacre, where more than 160 Cheyenne and Arapaho people - mostly elders, women, and children - were murdered by Colorado's Third Cavalry in November 1864, just six months after the University's founding. </p><p>Like many colleges and universities in the American West, DU has appropriated and romanticized Native American peoples and the U.S. settler colonial project. While many of our alumni associate the term \"pioneer\" with positive memories of the university, for many others, this term represents the forced expropriation of Native lands. Universities use mascots and nicknames to create a sense of community, but for Native students, alumni, faculty, staff, this term is a reminder of stolen land and genocide.</p><p>This exhibit explores DU's history of mascots, nicknames, and branding and the changes over its 150 year history, which often took place alongside related social and cultural changes. “Pioneer” has not always been the nickname and Denver Boone was not the first mascot. DU’s traditions have changed considerably over time, and these changes – like the demands to remove \"Pioneer\" as the nickname – have largely been student-driven.</p></div><div class=\"label-wrapper\"><div class=\"label\" style=\"margin: 50px auto 20px auto; width: 40%; text-align: center; font-size: 1.25em\"><hr style=\"height: 3px; color: #8D1737; opacity: 0.75; width: 55%; margin: 1rem auto 2rem auto;\"><div>DU is long overdue for another change.</div><br><div>#NoMorePios</div><hr style=\"height: 3px; color: #8D1737; opacity: 0.75; width: 55%; margin: 2rem auto 1rem auto;\"></div></div></div>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Founded in 1864, the University of Denver (DU) has a history of athletic team nicknames, mascots, and university branding that draw heavily on the identity as the oldest private university in Colorado. The history of DU is also forever connected to the Sand Creek Massacre, where more than 160 Cheyenne and Arapaho people - mostly elders, women, and children - were murdered by Colorado's Third Cavalry in November 1864, just six months after the University's founding. </p><p>Like many colleges and universities in the American West, DU has appropriated and romanticized Native American peoples and the U.S. settler colonial project. While many of our alumni associate the term \"pioneer\" with positive memories of the university, for many others, this term represents the forced expropriation of Native lands. Universities use mascots and nicknames to create a sense of community, but for Native students, alumni, faculty, staff, this term is a reminder of stolen land and genocide.</p><p>This exhibit explores DU's history of mascots, nicknames, and branding and the changes over its 150 year history, which often took place alongside related social and cultural changes. “Pioneer” has not always been the nickname and Denver Boone was not the first mascot. DU’s traditions have changed considerably over time, and these changes – like the demands to remove \"Pioneer\" as the nickname – have largely been student-driven.</p><p>DU is long overdue for another change.</p><br><p>#NoMorePios</p>"
--- tbl_exhibits.about_the_curators id=3
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;The curators of this exhibition exploring the impacts of DU mascots and nicknames on Native American students, faculty, staff, allies, and community would like to acknowledge how their own experiences and positionalities influenced the research and development of this exhibit. Each curator approached this topic as non-Native people who identify as white women. They recognize the complex relationships between Indigenous peoples and universities, the unequal power dynamics between non-Native academics and Indigenous communities, and the exploitation of Indigenous peoples, their representations, and their lands.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Kate Crowe (Associate Professor, Curator of Special Collections and Archives)&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Elia Trucks (Assistant Professor, Student Outreach Librarian)&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Madison Sussmann (Exhibits Manager)&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>The curators of this exhibition exploring the impacts of DU mascots and nicknames on Native American students, faculty, staff, allies, and community would like to acknowledge how their own experiences and positionalities influenced the research and development of this exhibit. Each curator approached this topic as non-Native people who identify as white women. They recognize the complex relationships between Indigenous peoples and universities, the unequal power dynamics between non-Native academics and Indigenous communities, and the exploitation of Indigenous peoples, their representations, and their lands.</p><p>Kate Crowe (Associate Professor, Curator of Special Collections and Archives)</p><p>Elia Trucks (Assistant Professor, Student Outreach Librarian)</p><p>Madison Sussmann (Exhibits Manager)</p>"

--- tbl_exhibits.description id=6
  BEFORE: "<p>Hanya Holm is popularly known for her innovative choreography in Broadway musicals like My Fair Lady and Kiss Me, Kate. She is also revered as one of the \"big four\" founders of modern dance, alongside Martha Graham, Doris Humphrey, and Charles Weidman.</p><p>Holm first came to Colorado in 1933 when she taught at the Perry Mansfield Dance Camp in Steamboat Springs. Beginning in 1941, she returned every summer for 43 years, making the state a destination for anyone who wanted to learn about modern dance.</p><p>Holm’s influence on dance in Colorado was extensive; she taught Freidann Parker and Lillian Covillo (co-founders of Colorado Ballet), Alfred Brooks and Maxine Munt (co-founders of the Changing Scene Theatre), Vera Sears (teacher and later director of the Children’s Dance Theatre at the University of Denver).</p><p>The exhibit features images of Holm throughout her career as a dancer, choreographer, and educator, as well as never-before-exhibited images of Holm in personal settings. The images in the exhibit are primarily from the collections of <a target=\"_blank\" href=\"https://specialcollections.du.edu/object/2c2c1422-a728-4a2a-9444-ceb0a11edf2d\">Maxine Munt</a> (a student of Holm) and <a target=\"_blank\" href=\"https://specialcollections.du.edu/object/2fbea091-2b7a-4456-b31d-8e5ed0d9bf46\">Marshall Brooks</a> (husband of one of Holm’s company members and close family friend).</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Hanya Holm is popularly known for her innovative choreography in Broadway musicals like My Fair Lady and Kiss Me, Kate. She is also revered as one of the \"big four\" founders of modern dance, alongside Martha Graham, Doris Humphrey, and Charles Weidman.</p><p>Holm first came to Colorado in 1933 when she taught at the Perry Mansfield Dance Camp in Steamboat Springs. Beginning in 1941, she returned every summer for 43 years, making the state a destination for anyone who wanted to learn about modern dance.</p><p>Holm’s influence on dance in Colorado was extensive; she taught Freidann Parker and Lillian Covillo (co-founders of Colorado Ballet), Alfred Brooks and Maxine Munt (co-founders of the Changing Scene Theatre), Vera Sears (teacher and later director of the Children’s Dance Theatre at the University of Denver).</p><p>The exhibit features images of Holm throughout her career as a dancer, choreographer, and educator, as well as never-before-exhibited images of Holm in personal settings. The images in the exhibit are primarily from the collections of <a target=\"_blank\" href=\"https://specialcollections.du.edu/object/2c2c1422-a728-4a2a-9444-ceb0a11edf2d\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Maxine Munt</a> (a student of Holm) and <a target=\"_blank\" href=\"https://specialcollections.du.edu/object/2fbea091-2b7a-4456-b31d-8e5ed0d9bf46\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Marshall Brooks</a> (husband of one of Holm’s company members and close family friend).</p>"

--- tbl_exhibits.about_the_curators id=8
  BEFORE: "Deborah Howard is a Professor of Painting at the University of Denver&#x27;s School of Art and Art History. She earned her Bachelor of Fine Arts from the Rhode Island School of Design and her Master of Fine Arts from the University of Wisconsin-Madison. With over four decades of experience in art education, Howard has taught drawing and painting at the university level.\n\nHer artistic work centers on themes of heritage, migration, memory, and hope. She is known for using worn shoes from various time periods in her installations, casting them in materials like iron, bronze, glass, and paper to symbolize the human presence and the passage of time."
  AFTER:  "<p>Deborah Howard is a Professor of Painting at the University of Denver's School of Art and Art History. She earned her Bachelor of Fine Arts from the Rhode Island School of Design and her Master of Fine Arts from the University of Wisconsin-Madison. With over four decades of experience in art education, Howard has taught drawing and painting at the university level.</p><p>Her artistic work centers on themes of heritage, migration, memory, and hope. She is known for using worn shoes from various time periods in her installations, casting them in materials like iron, bronze, glass, and paper to symbolize the human presence and the passage of time.</p>"
--- tbl_exhibits.description id=9
  BEFORE: "<p>Latinx poetry has been greatly ignored throughout history, as Spanish (in reference to Spain) writings have overshadowed other Spanish and indigenous languages’ presence in art. This research project is intended to introduce Latinx poetry to DU through an academic perspective, demonstrate how Nahuatl, the language spoken by the Aztecs, Spanish, and English have evolved together overtime through prose, and acknowledge the lasting emotions felt by the people who were forced to assimilate to American culture as a result of historic border movements across the south-west region of the United States. Furthermore, this project attempts to explore Latinx poetry through the perspective of analyzing relationships within linguistics to demonstrate how the intermingling between languages can result in valuable art and narratives.</p>\n\n<p>Throughout my research, I have found that Latinx poetry that incorporates multiple languages also breaks the barriers of historical stigmatization that is connected to Latinx culture and attempts to reclaim pride in tradition and linguistics; this is symbolic as it breaks away from the lasting effects of European colonization. This theme is very apparent throughout many artifacts and poems featured in this exhibit. Furthermore, because of this complex relationship, Nahuatl–a rarely spoken indigenous language–has remained relevant in the south-west region of the U.S., specifically in New Mexico, California, and Colorado, as it has used Spanish as a bridge into English-dominated arts. As Tlapoyawa explains, there is no “pure” Spanish language as it has evolved through the adoption of loanwords from both Nahuatl and English (Tlapoyawa 2023, 4-6). Therefore, this movement and evolution that the Spanish language has undergone should be celebrated and respected regardless of its country, region, or culture of origin.</p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Cover image: Mondragón, Sergio, and Margaret Randall, eds. 1963. <i>El Corno Emplumado: The Plumed Horn, no. 5 (January).<i> \"Fresco Teotihuacano.\" Courtesy DU Special Collections and Archives. Digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></i></p><i><i>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Tlapoyawa, Kurly. 2023. <i>Totacho: Our Way of Talking: The Nawatl Presence in New Mexico and Southern Colorado.</i> Albuquerque, NM: Mexika Press.</p></i></i>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Latinx poetry has been greatly ignored throughout history, as Spanish (in reference to Spain) writings have overshadowed other Spanish and indigenous languages’ presence in art. This research project is intended to introduce Latinx poetry to DU through an academic perspective, demonstrate how Nahuatl, the language spoken by the Aztecs, Spanish, and English have evolved together overtime through prose, and acknowledge the lasting emotions felt by the people who were forced to assimilate to American culture as a result of historic border movements across the south-west region of the United States. Furthermore, this project attempts to explore Latinx poetry through the perspective of analyzing relationships within linguistics to demonstrate how the intermingling between languages can result in valuable art and narratives.</p>\n\n<p>Throughout my research, I have found that Latinx poetry that incorporates multiple languages also breaks the barriers of historical stigmatization that is connected to Latinx culture and attempts to reclaim pride in tradition and linguistics; this is symbolic as it breaks away from the lasting effects of European colonization. This theme is very apparent throughout many artifacts and poems featured in this exhibit. Furthermore, because of this complex relationship, Nahuatl–a rarely spoken indigenous language–has remained relevant in the south-west region of the U.S., specifically in New Mexico, California, and Colorado, as it has used Spanish as a bridge into English-dominated arts. As Tlapoyawa explains, there is no “pure” Spanish language as it has evolved through the adoption of loanwords from both Nahuatl and English (Tlapoyawa 2023, 4-6). Therefore, this movement and evolution that the Spanish language has undergone should be celebrated and respected regardless of its country, region, or culture of origin.</p>\n\n<p>Cover image: Mondragón, Sergio, and Margaret Randall, eds. 1963. <i>El Corno Emplumado: The Plumed Horn, no. 5 (January).<i> \"Fresco Teotihuacano.\" Courtesy DU Special Collections and Archives. Digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></i></p><i><i>\n\n<p>Tlapoyawa, Kurly. 2023. <i>Totacho: Our Way of Talking: The Nawatl Presence in New Mexico and Southern Colorado.</i> Albuquerque, NM: Mexika Press.</p></i></i>"

--- tbl_exhibits.about_the_curators id=11
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Madison Sussmann is the Exhibits Librarian at the University of Denver Libraries, where she curates research-based exhibits that connect archives, storytelling, and public engagement. With a background in anthropology and museum studies, she focuses on making complex ideas accessible and visually compelling.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;She created Mars: Red Rocks to the Red Planet to highlight Colorado’s vital role in Mars exploration and to draw connections between the state’s iconic landscapes and the history of space science.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"

--- tbl_exhibits.description id=21
  BEFORE: "<i>Sazón & Liberation</i> was born from a belief that every person carries an inherent dignity, and that telling our stories—especially those pushed to the margins—is an act of liberation. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=18
  BEFORE: "<p>Maria Loewenstein suffered deep depression after Henry left for safety in England. The war had started and there was little hope that she would ever see her son again. She had been aware for some time that her husband, Dr. Max Loewenstein was addicted to morphine and she now insisted that something had to be done about it.</p>\r\n<p>Max’s addiction began as the result of a foot injury he sustained in 1933 while hiking with friends in the Harz Mountains in Southern Germany. Believing it to be a bad sprain, he refused X-rays and prescribed himself morphine for the pain. Too late, Max learned that his foot had been fractured and could no longer be set properly. Over the years, the break continued to cause Max pain, and he became addicted to the morphine. Maria had wanted Max committed to a drug rehabilitation facility for years.</p>\r\n<p>Finally, on September 25, 1941, Max agreed to admit himself to the Wittenauer Sanatorium in Berlin. Three weeks later, the Nazis began massive sweeps of Berlin’s Jews in which many, including Max’s brother Georg and sister-in-law Alice, were transported to the Łódź (Poland) and Riga (Latvia) ghettoes or to concentration camps in Poland. Many died there of starvation. Any survivors were later taken to Auschwitz and killed.</p>\r\n<p>Maria visited Max while he was in Wittenauer, but pretended not to know where her husband was when the Nazis came looking for him, endangering herself to protect him. Max was discharged from Wittenauer in April 1942. Thankfully, the Nazis’ push to remove Berlin’s Jews had temporarily slowed down. Those seven months saved his life, not only in treating his addiction to morphine, but from almost certain death at the hands of the Nazis.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Maria Loewenstein suffered deep depression after Henry left for safety in England. The war had started and there was little hope that she would ever see her son again. She had been aware for some time that her husband, Dr. Max Loewenstein was addicted to morphine and she now insisted that something had to be done about it.</p>\n<p>Max’s addiction began as the result of a foot injury he sustained in 1933 while hiking with friends in the Harz Mountains in Southern Germany. Believing it to be a bad sprain, he refused X-rays and prescribed himself morphine for the pain. Too late, Max learned that his foot had been fractured and could no longer be set properly. Over the years, the break continued to cause Max pain, and he became addicted to the morphine. Maria had wanted Max committed to a drug rehabilitation facility for years.</p>\n<p>Finally, on September 25, 1941, Max agreed to admit himself to the Wittenauer Sanatorium in Berlin. Three weeks later, the Nazis began massive sweeps of Berlin’s Jews in which many, including Max’s brother Georg and sister-in-law Alice, were transported to the Łódź (Poland) and Riga (Latvia) ghettoes or to concentration camps in Poland. Many died there of starvation. Any survivors were later taken to Auschwitz and killed.</p>\n<p>Maria visited Max while he was in Wittenauer, but pretended not to know where her husband was when the Nazis came looking for him, endangering herself to protect him. Max was discharged from Wittenauer in April 1942. Thankfully, the Nazis’ push to remove Berlin’s Jews had temporarily slowed down. Those seven months saved his life, not only in treating his addiction to morphine, but from almost certain death at the hands of the Nazis.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=24
  BEFORE: "<p>Throughout the War, many victims of the Nazi regime found ways to express their suffering through creative outlets. Maria Loewenstein, a professional artist and designer, expressed her feelings through her art.</p>\r\n<p>'JOB,' 1945, depicts the central figure in the Book of Job from the Bible. Job has long been a reference point in Jewish philosophy and religion, symbolizing the suffering of the Jewish people.</p>\r\n<p>'Despair' is far more literal. In it, a woman sits alone in a cold, dark, nearly bare apartment. She is huddled in front of a stove and wrapped heavily in blankets, her cheek resting on her hand. The basket next to her is empty, and the room's one shelf has no food, only a few cups and an unlit candle.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Throughout the War, many victims of the Nazi regime found ways to express their suffering through creative outlets. Maria Loewenstein, a professional artist and designer, expressed her feelings through her art.</p>\n<p>'JOB,' 1945, depicts the central figure in the Book of Job from the Bible. Job has long been a reference point in Jewish philosophy and religion, symbolizing the suffering of the Jewish people.</p>\n<p>'Despair' is far more literal. In it, a woman sits alone in a cold, dark, nearly bare apartment. She is huddled in front of a stove and wrapped heavily in blankets, her cheek resting on her hand. The basket next to her is empty, and the room's one shelf has no food, only a few cups and an unlit candle.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=25
  BEFORE: "<p>This poem written by Thekla Stoll in June 1942 was circulated among Germany's remaining Jews toward the end of the War. The poem is a testament to the Jewish people's spirit in the face of Nazi brutality.</p>\n<p>Today I saw 1,000 disturbed people.<br />Today I saw 1,000 Jews, wandering into oblivion,<br />Into the grey of the cold morning drew the condemned<br />Leaving behind what once was their life.<br /><br />They stepped through the gates, glancing back,<br />As they left everything outside, their homeland, their belongings and their happiness,<br />Where will you be led, where ends your path?<br />They only know this: The destination is called barbed wire!<br /><br />And what awaits you is misery, torture and distress,<br />Suffering, hunger, disease; for many soon-to-follow: death.<br />I search your eyes with a brother’s look<br />Expecting deepest grief in this misfortune.<br /><br />Instead of despair one sees a deep, deep strength.<br />And bearing and composure glows in their eyes<br />One sees a burning will to live, sees faith and courage<br />And sees in some faces a smile, strong and good.<br /><br />I am strongly moved to recognize the spirit of the people,<br />Selected for suffering that will be overcome,<br />That lifts itself out of misery and need, banishment, forced labor<br />And imprisonment, with unbroken strength.<br /><br />Today I saw 1,000 people with disturbed faces,<br />And saw in the grey of the morning the rays of everlasting light!<br /></p>\n<p><i>Translated by Henry Lowenstein</i></p>\n<p><i>Thekla Stoll was deported to Auschwitz 3 Mar 1943</i></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>This poem written by Thekla Stoll in June 1942 was circulated among Germany's remaining Jews toward the end of the War. The poem is a testament to the Jewish people's spirit in the face of Nazi brutality.</p>\n<p>Today I saw 1,000 disturbed people.<br>Today I saw 1,000 Jews, wandering into oblivion,<br>Into the grey of the cold morning drew the condemned<br>Leaving behind what once was their life.<br><br>They stepped through the gates, glancing back,<br>As they left everything outside, their homeland, their belongings and their happiness,<br>Where will you be led, where ends your path?<br>They only know this: The destination is called barbed wire!<br><br>And what awaits you is misery, torture and distress,<br>Suffering, hunger, disease; for many soon-to-follow: death.<br>I search your eyes with a brother’s look<br>Expecting deepest grief in this misfortune.<br><br>Instead of despair one sees a deep, deep strength.<br>And bearing and composure glows in their eyes<br>One sees a burning will to live, sees faith and courage<br>And sees in some faces a smile, strong and good.<br><br>I am strongly moved to recognize the spirit of the people,<br>Selected for suffering that will be overcome,<br>That lifts itself out of misery and need, banishment, forced labor<br>And imprisonment, with unbroken strength.<br><br>Today I saw 1,000 people with disturbed faces,<br>And saw in the grey of the morning the rays of everlasting light!<br></p>\n<p><i>Translated by Henry Lowenstein</i></p>\n<p><i>Thekla Stoll was deported to Auschwitz 3 Mar 1943</i></p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=30
  BEFORE: "<p>Under Soviet pressure Grotewohl met secretly in February/March 1946 with Wilhelm Pieck, the leader of the German Communists. Karin was the only other person present and was asked to take notes as the two leaders planned to betray the Western powers. After the meeting she made an extra copy of the notes and passed them to her American contacts. The next day Grotewohl sent his limousine to take her to work. Suddenly the limousine was surrounded by Russian soldiers, intending to deliver her to the Soviet headquarters. The soldiers had never seen such a car and made the chauffer drive them around to show off the limousine to their comrades, enjoying a drink at every stop. Fortunately at one stop the soldier left behind to guard Karin got annoyed when his buddies did not come out and he went looking for them. Karin’s driver seized this opportunity and drove immediately to her American friends.</p>\r\n<p>It turned out that the information Karin had passed on, had been leaked and was the front page story in the morning newspaper. A secretary, who worked with Karin, but had known nothing of Karin’s actions, had already been arrested and executed.</p>\r\n<p>Once again the Loewenstein family was in danger, but this time they had powerful allies. The Americans speeded up the immigration paperwork for Karin, Maria, and Max and protected them until they could depart for safety in the United States.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Under Soviet pressure Grotewohl met secretly in February/March 1946 with Wilhelm Pieck, the leader of the German Communists. Karin was the only other person present and was asked to take notes as the two leaders planned to betray the Western powers. After the meeting she made an extra copy of the notes and passed them to her American contacts. The next day Grotewohl sent his limousine to take her to work. Suddenly the limousine was surrounded by Russian soldiers, intending to deliver her to the Soviet headquarters. The soldiers had never seen such a car and made the chauffer drive them around to show off the limousine to their comrades, enjoying a drink at every stop. Fortunately at one stop the soldier left behind to guard Karin got annoyed when his buddies did not come out and he went looking for them. Karin’s driver seized this opportunity and drove immediately to her American friends.</p>\n<p>It turned out that the information Karin had passed on, had been leaked and was the front page story in the morning newspaper. A secretary, who worked with Karin, but had known nothing of Karin’s actions, had already been arrested and executed.</p>\n<p>Once again the Loewenstein family was in danger, but this time they had powerful allies. The Americans speeded up the immigration paperwork for Karin, Maria, and Max and protected them until they could depart for safety in the United States.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p>During his eight years in England, Henry worked at the Whipsnade Zoo and at a nearby farm and studied art. Henry was issued an immigration visa to the United States on April 30, 1947. A week later he purchased his ticket on the onetime troop transport, the SS Marine Falcon, from the J. D. Hewett Travel Agency in London.</p>\r\n<p>The SS Marine Falcon sailed from Southampton, England on June 12, 1947 and arrived in New York, New York on June 21, a year after his family’s move to Pennsylvania and just in time for Henry's twenty-second birthday. Henry was reunited with his parents Max, Maria, and his sister Karin. The family's long nightmare of persecution and separation was finally over.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>During his eight years in England, Henry worked at the Whipsnade Zoo and at a nearby farm and studied art. Henry was issued an immigration visa to the United States on April 30, 1947. A week later he purchased his ticket on the onetime troop transport, the SS Marine Falcon, from the J. D. Hewett Travel Agency in London.</p>\n<p>The SS Marine Falcon sailed from Southampton, England on June 12, 1947 and arrived in New York, New York on June 21, a year after his family’s move to Pennsylvania and just in time for Henry's twenty-second birthday. Henry was reunited with his parents Max, Maria, and his sister Karin. The family's long nightmare of persecution and separation was finally over.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=40
  BEFORE: "<div><div><p>The University began to reckon with its role in and responsibility for the Sand Creek Massacre in the years leading up to the 150th anniversary of both the University and the Massacre in 2014. A committee of faculty and staff from DU and other institutions was created to inquire into the nature of John Evans’s involvement in the Sand Creek Massacre. The University of Denver \"John Evans Study Committee Report\" was produced, and the study found John Evans culpable in the Sand Creek Massacre. This report and some of the recommendations from the Native American Inclusivity Task Force (2016) helped implement some modest structural change, but these changes have proven to be inconsistent and incomplete. The Native Student Alliance (NSA) has been the primary force behind the most recent demands to remove both the \"Boone\" character and the \"Pioneer\" moniker, as both are legacies of the U.S. and DU’s history with settler colonialism.</p><p>As so many communities reckon with the presence and prominence of statues, memorials, flags, and other signifiers of our racist and colonial history, the University of Denver has a choice:</p></div><div class=\"label-wrapper\"><div class=\"label\" style=\"margin: 50px auto 20px auto; width: 40%; text-align: center; font-size: 1.25em\"><hr style=\"height: 3px; color: #8D1737; opacity: 0.75; width: 55%; margin: 1rem auto 2rem auto;\"><div>Which side of history do we want to be on?</div><hr style=\"height: 3px; color: #8D1737; opacity: 0.75; width: 55%; margin: 2rem auto 1rem auto;\"></div></div></div>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The University began to reckon with its role in and responsibility for the Sand Creek Massacre in the years leading up to the 150th anniversary of both the University and the Massacre in 2014. A committee of faculty and staff from DU and other institutions was created to inquire into the nature of John Evans’s involvement in the Sand Creek Massacre. The University of Denver \"John Evans Study Committee Report\" was produced, and the study found John Evans culpable in the Sand Creek Massacre. This report and some of the recommendations from the Native American Inclusivity Task Force (2016) helped implement some modest structural change, but these changes have proven to be inconsistent and incomplete. The Native Student Alliance (NSA) has been the primary force behind the most recent demands to remove both the \"Boone\" character and the \"Pioneer\" moniker, as both are legacies of the U.S. and DU’s history with settler colonialism.</p><p>As so many communities reckon with the presence and prominence of statues, memorials, flags, and other signifiers of our racist and colonial history, the University of Denver has a choice:</p><p>Which side of history do we want to be on?</p>"

  BEFORE: "<div class=\"label-wrapper\"><div class=\"label\" style=\"margin: 0px auto 20px auto; width: 52%; text-align: center; font-size: 1.25em; line-height: 1.6em\"><hr style=\"height: 3px; color: #8D1737; opacity: 0.75; width: 55%; margin: 1rem auto 2rem auto;\"><div>While the Pioneer moniker persists, continued harm to the original inhabitants of the land that the University of Denver sits on, and all Indigenous peoples, is inevitable.</div><hr style=\"height: 3px; color: #8D1737; opacity: 0.75; width: 55%; margin: 2rem auto 1rem auto;\"></div></div>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=43
  BEFORE: "<div>Mascots that uphold and reinforce settler colonial stereotypes can have negative psychological ramifications for Native youth. Stephanie Fryberg (Tulalip), writing with several co-authors (2010) found that after viewing mascots and “romantic” movies like Pocahontas, Native youth had lower self-esteem and sense of community worth (2010). Davis-Delano, Gone, and Fryberg (2020) explored the psychosocial effects of mascots on Native youth through a comprehensive review. They found that Native American mascots, regardless of the stated intent to “honor” Native Americans, “induce or correlate with negative psychosocial outcomes.” There was no evidence from any study that Native American mascots foster positive or beneficial psychosocial effects for Native Americans.” (Davis-Delano et al., 2020).  The use of mascots and other romanticized views of Native Americans and the American West maintain the harmful stereotype that Native Americans are part of the historicized past, rather than contemporary people who are part of sovereign nations that continue to survive and resist. </div><div>As the University re-engages the Native American Community Advisory board and continues down the path of reconciliation with the Northern Cheyenne, Northern Arapaho and Southern Cheyenne and Arapaho Nations, it is important that senior leadership reckon honestly and transparently with the impacts of our decisions about messaging and representation in regards to the mascot and Pioneer nickname.</div>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Mascots that uphold and reinforce settler colonial stereotypes can have negative psychological ramifications for Native youth. Stephanie Fryberg (Tulalip), writing with several co-authors (2010) found that after viewing mascots and “romantic” movies like Pocahontas, Native youth had lower self-esteem and sense of community worth (2010). Davis-Delano, Gone, and Fryberg (2020) explored the psychosocial effects of mascots on Native youth through a comprehensive review. They found that Native American mascots, regardless of the stated intent to “honor” Native Americans, “induce or correlate with negative psychosocial outcomes.” There was no evidence from any study that Native American mascots foster positive or beneficial psychosocial effects for Native Americans.” (Davis-Delano et al., 2020).  The use of mascots and other romanticized views of Native Americans and the American West maintain the harmful stereotype that Native Americans are part of the historicized past, rather than contemporary people who are part of sovereign nations that continue to survive and resist. </p><p>As the University re-engages the Native American Community Advisory board and continues down the path of reconciliation with the Northern Cheyenne, Northern Arapaho and Southern Cheyenne and Arapaho Nations, it is important that senior leadership reckon honestly and transparently with the impacts of our decisions about messaging and representation in regards to the mascot and Pioneer nickname.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p>High-resolution, large scale assemblage prints created from the Denver Museum of Nature and Science’s extinct bird collection lined\nthe walls of the reading area on the upper level. Weaver placed prints of the Eskimo Curlew, Carolina Parakeet, and Imperial\nWoodpecker between the library’s large windows in order to integrate them with the campus’ visual space.</p>\n\n<p>Dr. Timothy Weaver is a new media artist and former life scientist whose ambition is to contribute to the restoration of ecological\nmemory through a process of speculative inquiry along the art/technology interface. Weaver’s live cinema, video and sound-art\nprojects, and interactive installations have been featured at the Denver Art Museum, FILE Hipersonica (Brazil), Transmediale (Berlin),\nSubtleTechnologies Festival (Toronto), New Forms Festival (Vancouver), Korean Experimental Art Festival, Boston CyberArts,\nSIGGRAPH, and the New York Digital Salon. Weaver is currently an associate professor and director of Emergent Digital Practices\n(EDP) in the School of Art and Art History at the University of Denver.</p>\n\n<p>The juxtaposition of these extinct species against the University of Denver's vibrant outdoor\nlandscape heightens the dichotomy between the natural world as it once existed and how we\nexperience it today.</p>\n\n<button style=\"background-color: #B4CC5A; color: #ffffff; padding: 10px 20px; border: none; border-radius: 4px; font-size: 16px; cursor: pointer;\">MORE INFORMATION ABOUT THE ARTIST</button>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=67
  BEFORE: "Hanya Holm's dance career began in Germany, where she studied with, taught for, and eventually co-directed for Mary Wigman, the leading German modern dancer in the 1920s. Holm became Wigman's chief instructor in 1928.</p><p>Sol Hurok, a dance impresario who hoped to promote a Wigman company in America, offered to finance Holm’s move to the United States in 1931. Holm, who was unnerved by the rise of Adolf Hitler, eagerly accepted his offer. She would spend the early 1930s teaching the Wigman technique at her New York-based school, renaming it in 1936 (with Wigman's permission) to the Hanya Holm School of Dance.</p><p>These early years in America would influence Holm's move away from the darker, angst-ridden, European Wigman technique to a freer, more American style of modern dance.</p>"
  AFTER:  "Hanya Holm's dance career began in Germany, where she studied with, taught for, and eventually co-directed for Mary Wigman, the leading German modern dancer in the 1920s. Holm became Wigman's chief instructor in 1928.<p></p><p>Sol Hurok, a dance impresario who hoped to promote a Wigman company in America, offered to finance Holm’s move to the United States in 1931. Holm, who was unnerved by the rise of Adolf Hitler, eagerly accepted his offer. She would spend the early 1930s teaching the Wigman technique at her New York-based school, renaming it in 1936 (with Wigman's permission) to the Hanya Holm School of Dance.</p><p>These early years in America would influence Holm's move away from the darker, angst-ridden, European Wigman technique to a freer, more American style of modern dance.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=89
  BEFORE: "<p>Mary Morris Vaux was born in 1860 in Philadelphia, PA. During annual family trips to the Canadian Rockies beginning in 1887, she became a mountain climber, outdoorswoman, photographer, and watercolor painter. In 1913, she climbed Mount Robson, the highest peak in the British Columbia Rockies, and in 1915 she married Charles Doolittle Walcott, a paleontologist and Secretary of the Smithsonian Institute. They met in the field while Walcott searched for invertebrate fossils in the Burgess Shale. Mary Vaux Walcott continued to travel to the Rocky Mountains every summer well into her old age.</p>\n\n<p>Between 1925 and 1928, Mary Vaux Walcott published four hundred drawings in five volumes of North American Wild Flowers. They were sold as part of her husband’s fund-raising campaign, and all proceeds went to the Smithsonian Institute’s endowment. Mount Mary Vaux in Jasper National Park in Alberta, Canada, was named in her honor.</p>:\n\n<p><b>Below is a <i>North American Wildflowers, Volume 1</i>, and six prints from the volume from DU Libraries' Special Collections & Archives.</b> </p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Mary Morris Vaux was born in 1860 in Philadelphia, PA. During annual family trips to the Canadian Rockies beginning in 1887, she became a mountain climber, outdoorswoman, photographer, and watercolor painter. In 1913, she climbed Mount Robson, the highest peak in the British Columbia Rockies, and in 1915 she married Charles Doolittle Walcott, a paleontologist and Secretary of the Smithsonian Institute. They met in the field while Walcott searched for invertebrate fossils in the Burgess Shale. Mary Vaux Walcott continued to travel to the Rocky Mountains every summer well into her old age.</p>\n\n<p>Between 1925 and 1928, Mary Vaux Walcott published four hundred drawings in five volumes of North American Wild Flowers. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=119
  BEFORE: "<p>On April 4th 2016, the anniversary of Martin Luther King Jr’s murder, the Black Graduate Student Alliance (BGSA) & Student Advocates for Instilling Change (SAIC) hosted a Die-In. This Die-In was done to bring awareness to the physical and psychological implications of racism across the United States.</p>\n\n<p>The Die-In lasted for 16 minutes, with every minute representing a bullet that Laquan McDonald took from a Chicago PD officer. The idea stemmed from graduate psychology students in Chicago, and through media and networking, it reached DU’s BGSA and SAIC. Being at the Anderson Academic Commons, not only did the Chancellor see this demonstration, but the building’s centrality on campus ensured that passing students saw it as well.</p>\n\n<p><b>#psychologists4blacklives</b></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>On April 4th 2016, the anniversary of Martin Luther King Jr’s murder, the Black Graduate Student Alliance (BGSA) &amp; Student Advocates for Instilling Change (SAIC) hosted a Die-In. This Die-In was done to bring awareness to the physical and psychological implications of racism across the United States.</p>\n\n<p>The Die-In lasted for 16 minutes, with every minute representing a bullet that Laquan McDonald took from a Chicago PD officer. The idea stemmed from graduate psychology students in Chicago, and through media and networking, it reached DU’s BGSA and SAIC. Being at the Anderson Academic Commons, not only did the Chancellor see this demonstration, but the building’s centrality on campus ensured that passing students saw it as well.</p>\n\n<p><b>#psychologists4blacklives</b></p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=121
  BEFORE: "<p>The Blackout for Black Lives followed closely on the heels of the protest at the Freedom of Speech Wall, and “occurred in response to the racialized violence and silencing occurring at the University of Denver (DU).” Individuals were asked to wear black as part of the silent protest. It successfully brought issues at DU to light and challenged DU as an institution to take action on these issues.</p>\n\n<p>The blackout and demonstration gained the participation of not only DU community members & administration, but individuals across the entire United States of America. The silent protest took place on the Driscoll Bridge, with a 10-minute moment of silence to honor victims of police brutality.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The Blackout for Black Lives followed closely on the heels of the protest at the Freedom of Speech Wall, and “occurred in response to the racialized violence and silencing occurring at the University of Denver (DU).” Individuals were asked to wear black as part of the silent protest. It successfully brought issues at DU to light and challenged DU as an institution to take action on these issues.</p>\n\n<p>The blackout and demonstration gained the participation of not only DU community members &amp; administration, but individuals across the entire United States of America. The silent protest took place on the Driscoll Bridge, with a 10-minute moment of silence to honor victims of police brutality.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><b>\"In Search of the Lost Culture\"</b><br>\nLaurette Sejourne<br>\nJanuary 1963<br></p>\n\n<p>Two versions of the Aztec god of Ice, Iztlacoliuhqui, is paired with a piece of prose that has been translated from Spanish to English. This language translation offers a look into the duality of Aztec religion and culture through different perspectives (Spanish and English). This excerpt explores the pre Columbian Aztec reign, by recognizing the loss of its culture and tradition to Spanish colonization, resulting in a lost “true” understanding of the people (Sejourne 1956,18-19).</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of El Corno Emplumado, digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Séjourné, Laurette. 1956. <i>Burning Water: Thought and Religion in Ancient Mexico.</i> London: Thames and Hudson.</p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=144
  BEFORE: "<p><b>\"Día de Muertos\"</b><br>\nJuan Bañuelos<br>\nJanuary 1963</p>\n\n<p>The presented Aztec illustration offers, what seems to be, the image of a skeletal being devouring a person. The poem, titled \"Día de Muertos\" or Day of the Dead, paired with this figure explains that this describes the process of death (Bañuelos 1963, 31). The Day of the Dead is a celebration originating from the combination of Aztec/Nahua and European cultures, honoring those who have died and moved into the Chicunamictlán, or the Land of the Dead.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of </i>El Corno Emplumado<i>, digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Bañuelos, Juan. 1963. “Día de muertos.” <i>El Corno Emplumado</i> no. 5 (January): 31–32. Courtesy DU Special Collections and Archives; digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.<p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><b>\"Día de Muertos\"</b><br>\nJuan Bañuelos<br>\nJanuary 1963</p>\n\n<p>The presented Aztec illustration offers, what seems to be, the image of a skeletal being devouring a person. The poem, titled \"Día de Muertos\" or Day of the Dead, paired with this figure explains that this describes the process of death (Bañuelos 1963, 31). The Day of the Dead is a celebration originating from the combination of Aztec/Nahua and European cultures, honoring those who have died and moved into the Chicunamictlán, or the Land of the Dead.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of </i>El Corno Emplumado<i>, digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>\n\n<p>Bañuelos, Juan. 1963. “Día de muertos.” <i>El Corno Emplumado</i> no. 5 (January): 31–32. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=145
  BEFORE: "<p><b><i>El Corno Emplumado 5</i></b><br>\nSergio Mondragón & Margaret Randall<br>\nJanuary 1963</p>\n\n<p>Published in 1963, <i>El Corno Emplumado 5</i>, is a bilingual magazine that features prose and poetry from a multitude of authors all over the world. As the editors explain, the magazine means to break boundaries and explore the human experience of finding oneself through poetry and language (Mondragón and Randall 1963, 6). Whether intentional or not, this magazine has provided an inclusive celebration of many cultures, and art, acknowledging the importance of remembering harsh moments in history.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of </i>El Corno Emplumado<i>, digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\"> Mondragón, Sergio, and Margaret Randall, eds. 1963. <i>El Corno Emplumado:</i> The Plumed Horn, no. 5 (January). Digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><b><i>El Corno Emplumado 5</i></b><br>\nSergio Mondragón &amp; Margaret Randall<br>\nJanuary 1963</p>\n\n<p>Published in 1963, <i>El Corno Emplumado 5</i>, is a bilingual magazine that features prose and poetry from a multitude of authors all over the world. As the editors explain, the magazine means to break boundaries and explore the human experience of finding oneself through poetry and language (Mondragón and Randall 1963, 6). Whether intentional or not, this magazine has provided an inclusive celebration of many cultures, and art, acknowledging the importance of remembering harsh moments in history.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of </i>El Corno Emplumado<i>, digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>\n\n<p> Mondragón, Sergio, and Margaret Randall, eds. 1963. <i>El Corno Emplumado:</i> The Plumed Horn, no. 5 (January). Digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=146
  BEFORE: "<p><b><i>Spanish Folk-Poetry In New Mexico</i></b><br>\nArthur Leon Campa<br>\n1946</p>\n\n<p>Written in 1946, the book, <i>Spanish Folk-Poetry in New Mexico</i>, analyzes the origins of traditional Latinx songs and poems from different time periods. Campa explains that the encomienda system and territorial conflicts pushed the Nahua to assimilate to new European cultures, and languages merged and were integrated into these regions (1946, 7-12). As a result, New Mexico is the first and only state to have bilingual laws (Campa 1946, 12). These songs mark the incorporations of Nahua and European influences in Latinx culture.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of University of Denver's Special Collections and Archives.</i></p></b></b>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Campa, Arthur Leon. 1946. <i>Spanish Folk-Poetry in New Mexico.</i> Albuquerque: University of New Mexico Press.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><b><i>Spanish Folk-Poetry In New Mexico</i></b><br>\nArthur Leon Campa<br>\n1946</p>\n\n<p>Written in 1946, the book, <i>Spanish Folk-Poetry in New Mexico</i>, analyzes the origins of traditional Latinx songs and poems from different time periods. Campa explains that the encomienda system and territorial conflicts pushed the Nahua to assimilate to new European cultures, and languages merged and were integrated into these regions (1946, 7-12). As a result, New Mexico is the first and only state to have bilingual laws (Campa 1946, 12). These songs mark the incorporations of Nahua and European influences in Latinx culture.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of University of Denver's Special Collections and Archives.</i></p>\n\n<p>Campa, Arthur Leon. 1946. <i>Spanish Folk-Poetry in New Mexico.</i> Albuquerque: University of New Mexico Press.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=148
  BEFORE: "<p><b><i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche</i></b><br>\nFrancisco X. Alarcón<br>\n2002</p>\n\n<p>Alarcón features Nahuatl, Spanish, and English poetry in <i>From the Other Side of the Night / Del otro lado de la noche</i>. By playing the role of “activist” in the Snake Poems section, Alarcón calls attention to issues impacting the Latinx belonging in the U.S., related to topics of labor, gender, and oppression (Alarcón 2002, 199-200). He argues that acknowledging the oppression experienced by the Aztecs is the way to truly understand the modern sociocultural suffering experienced by Latinx communities.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of University of Denver Libraries.</i></p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Alarcón, Francisco X. 2002. <i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche.</i> Tucson: University of Arizona Press.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><b><i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche</i></b><br>\nFrancisco X. Alarcón<br>\n2002</p>\n\n<p>Alarcón features Nahuatl, Spanish, and English poetry in <i>From the Other Side of the Night / Del otro lado de la noche</i>. By playing the role of “activist” in the Snake Poems section, Alarcón calls attention to issues impacting the Latinx belonging in the U.S., related to topics of labor, gender, and oppression (Alarcón 2002, 199-200). He argues that acknowledging the oppression experienced by the Aztecs is the way to truly understand the modern sociocultural suffering experienced by Latinx communities.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of University of Denver Libraries.</i></p>\n\n<p>Alarcón, Francisco X. 2002. <i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche.</i> Tucson: University of Arizona Press.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=149
  BEFORE: "<p><b>\"Traveler's Prayer\"</b><br>\nFrancisco X. Alarcón<br>\n2002</p>\n\n<p>\"Traveler’s Prayer\" is a bilingual poem that creates a bridge between English and Nahuatl as it seems to explore the mindset of a Latinx adventurer who connects with Quetzalcoatl, the “Plumed Serpent” God from Aztec culture; the conqueror Hernán Cortés impersonated Quetzalcoatl to gain access into Aztec territory (Alarcón 2002, 209). These historical references can help explain how the Latinx community views their place in the U.S. as the lasting generational trauma is rooted in colonization and forced assimilation.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of University of Denver Libraries.</i></p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Alarcón, Francisco X. 2002. “Traveler’s Prayer.” In <i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche: New and Selected Poems</i>, 33. Tucson: University of Arizona Press.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><b>\"Traveler's Prayer\"</b><br>\nFrancisco X. Alarcón<br>\n2002</p>\n\n<p>\"Traveler’s Prayer\" is a bilingual poem that creates a bridge between English and Nahuatl as it seems to explore the mindset of a Latinx adventurer who connects with Quetzalcoatl, the “Plumed Serpent” God from Aztec culture; the conqueror Hernán Cortés impersonated Quetzalcoatl to gain access into Aztec territory (Alarcón 2002, 209). These historical references can help explain how the Latinx community views their place in the U.S. as the lasting generational trauma is rooted in colonization and forced assimilation.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy of University of Denver Libraries.</i></p>\n\n<p>Alarcón, Francisco X. 2002. “Traveler’s Prayer.” In <i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche: New and Selected Poems</i>, 33. Tucson: University of Arizona Press.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=150
  BEFORE: "<p><b><i>El Corno Emplumado 5</i></b><br>\nSergio Mondragón & Margaret Randall<br>\nJanuary 1963</p>\n\n<p><i>El Corno Emplumado</i> is a bilingual journal with published prose and poetry in English and Spanish. The featured piece titled <i>Fresco Teotihuacano</i> presents Aztec art with imagery that can be interpreted as Gods or animalistic-mythical creatures.\nThroughout history, the Mexican American border has crossed a population native to the land, descendants of the Aztecs, the Nahua. As a result, cultural confusion and anger is present in these communities and is reflected in the poem <i>Desencapsulamiento</i>.</p>\n\n<p>Aztec culture continues to influence Latinx artistic expression in diverse ways. This journal demonstrates how cultures, and by extension languages, are bridged together over time. Nahuatl, Spanish, and English coexist both in this journal of prose and the real world, specifically in the south-west region of the United States. The intermingling between these three languages illustrates the significance for diversity of language and its influence on art and culture—connecting the past to the present.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy DU Special Collections and Archives, and digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><b><i>El Corno Emplumado 5</i></b><br>\nSergio Mondragón &amp; Margaret Randall<br>\nJanuary 1963</p>\n\n<p><i>El Corno Emplumado</i> is a bilingual journal with published prose and poetry in English and Spanish. The featured piece titled <i>Fresco Teotihuacano</i> presents Aztec art with imagery that can be interpreted as Gods or animalistic-mythical creatures.\nThroughout history, the Mexican American border has crossed a population native to the land, descendants of the Aztecs, the Nahua. As a result, cultural confusion and anger is present in these communities and is reflected in the poem <i>Desencapsulamiento</i>.</p>\n\n<p>Aztec culture continues to influence Latinx artistic expression in diverse ways. This journal demonstrates how cultures, and by extension languages, are bridged together over time. Nahuatl, Spanish, and English coexist both in this journal of prose and the real world, specifically in the south-west region of the United States. The intermingling between these three languages illustrates the significance for diversity of language and its influence on art and culture—connecting the past to the present.</p>\n\n<p><i>Courtesy DU Special Collections and Archives, and digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</i></p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=151
  BEFORE: "El Corno Emplumado 5\nSergio Mondragón & Margaret Randall\nJanuary 1963\n\nEl Corno Emplumado is a bilingual journal with published prose and poetry in English and Spanish. The featured piece titled Fresco Teotihuacano presents Aztec art with imagery that can be interpreted as Gods or animalistic-mythical creatures.\nThroughout history, the Mexican American border has crossed a population native to the land, descendants of the Aztecs, the Nahua. As a result, cultural confusion and anger is present in these communities and is reflected in the poem Desencapsulamiento.\n\nAztec culture continues to influence Latinx artistic expression in diverse ways. This journal demonstrates how cultures, and by extension languages, are bridged together over time. Nahuatl, Spanish, and English coexist both in this journal of prose and the real world, specifically in the south-west region of the United States. The intermingling between these three languages illustrates the significance for diversity of language and its influence on art and culture—connecting the past to the present.\n\nCourtesy DU Special Collections and Archives, and digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University."
  AFTER:  "<p>El Corno Emplumado 5<br>Sergio Mondragón &amp; Margaret Randall<br>January 1963</p><p>El Corno Emplumado is a bilingual journal with published prose and poetry in English and Spanish. The featured piece titled Fresco Teotihuacano presents Aztec art with imagery that can be interpreted as Gods or animalistic-mythical creatures.<br>Throughout history, the Mexican American border has crossed a population native to the land, descendants of the Aztecs, the Nahua. As a result, cultural confusion and anger is present in these communities and is reflected in the poem Desencapsulamiento.</p><p>Aztec culture continues to influence Latinx artistic expression in diverse ways. This journal demonstrates how cultures, and by extension languages, are bridged together over time. Nahuatl, Spanish, and English coexist both in this journal of prose and the real world, specifically in the south-west region of the United States. The intermingling between these three languages illustrates the significance for diversity of language and its influence on art and culture—connecting the past to the present.</p><p>Courtesy DU Special Collections and Archives, and digital version provided by Open Door Archives of Northwestern University.</p>"

The Rio Grande was the official border between the United States and Mexico (National Archives). As a result, the Mexican-American border crossed a population, merging languages and cultures.</p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">National Archives. n.d. “Teaching With Documents: The Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo.”. https://www.archives.gov/education/lessons/guadalupe-hidalgo.</p>\n\n<p style=\"color: #b23123; font-size: 150%;\"><b>1965-1975</b></p>\n<p><b>The Chicano Movement</b>: This movement was led by people of Mexican decent which advocated for civil rights, specifically labor, education, and economic rights (Facing History and Ourselves).</p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Facing History and Ourselves. n.d. “Background on the Chicano Movement.” https://www.facinghistory.org/resource-library/background-chicano-movement.</p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=153
  BEFORE: "<p>After the Spanish conquered the Aztecs, Spanish and Nahuatl began to mix. As both languages mixed they strengthened both their presence across Mexico and the south-west region of the U.S. as other indigenous languages became extinct due to cultural diffusion (Castillo 1963, 1-2). However, this process occurred overtime and due to some minor similarities between the two languages, the accentuation and vocabulary of both were able to connect in grammatical and structural ways creating loanwords (Castillo 1963, 4-5). This diffusion between both cultures and languages has resulted in this lasting relationship between Nahuatl and Spanish that has both greatly changed and protected these groups. However, the extent of this divergence between Nahuatl and Spanish has not been explored enough academically (Castillo 1963, 12).</p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Díaz del Castillo, Bernal. 1963. <i>The Conquest of New Spain.</i> Translated and with an introduction by John M. Cohen. Classics S. London: Penguin Books.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>After the Spanish conquered the Aztecs, Spanish and Nahuatl began to mix. As both languages mixed they strengthened both their presence across Mexico and the south-west region of the U.S. as other indigenous languages became extinct due to cultural diffusion (Castillo 1963, 1-2). However, this process occurred overtime and due to some minor similarities between the two languages, the accentuation and vocabulary of both were able to connect in grammatical and structural ways creating loanwords (Castillo 1963, 4-5). This diffusion between both cultures and languages has resulted in this lasting relationship between Nahuatl and Spanish that has both greatly changed and protected these groups. However, the extent of this divergence between Nahuatl and Spanish has not been explored enough academically (Castillo 1963, 12).</p>\n\n<p>Díaz del Castillo, Bernal. 1963. <i>The Conquest of New Spain.</i> Translated and with an introduction by John M. Cohen. Classics S. London: Penguin Books.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><b>Nahuatl/Nawatl:</b> Indigenous language spoken by the Aztecs, Toltecs, Pipiles, and more indigenous groups. This language continues to be spoken in the south-west region of the United States and areas of Mexico (Alarcón 2002, 208).</p>\n\n<p><b>Iztlacoliuhqui:</b> Aztec God of Ice</p>\n\n<p><b>Chicunamictlán:</b> Land of the dead</p>\n\n<p><b>Quetzalcoatl:</b> The Plumed Serpent Aztec God. He was impersonated by Hernán Cortés to gain access to the Aztec territories. Related to the God of Wind (Alarcón 2002, 209).</p>\n\n<p style=\"font-size: 80%;\">Alarcón, Francisco X. 2002. <i>From the Other Side of Night / Del otro lado de la noche.</i> Tucson: University of Arizona Press.</p>"

  BEFORE: "Irene Miller Stein was born in Denver, Colo. in 1894. She was the daughter of Anna and Robert Lazar Miller, both Lithuanian immigrant pioneers of Denver's Jewish community. Irene grew up in Denver and attended the Art Institute of Chicago. In 1921, she married Azriel Stein, whom with his brother founded, owned, and operated Pencol Drug Store on the corner of Pennsylvania Street and Colfax Avenue in Denver. Irene and Azriel's son Stanley was born in 1928. \n\nIrene Stein was a professional artist well into her 90s. She worked in many different media and styles, from art deco in the 1920s and 1930s to modern, abstract sculptures and computer-generated art in the 1970s and 1980s."
  AFTER:  "<p>Irene Miller Stein was born in Denver, Colo. in 1894. She was the daughter of Anna and Robert Lazar Miller, both Lithuanian immigrant pioneers of Denver's Jewish community. She worked in many different media and styles, from art deco in the 1920s and 1930s to modern, abstract sculptures and computer-generated art in the 1970s and 1980s.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p style=\"color: #794628; font-size: 120%;\"><b>The Chamberlin Observatory at the University of Denver, Colorado</b></p>\n\n<p>The Chamberlin Observatory at the University of Denver started observing Mars and other heavenly bodies in 1894. It was that year that the observatory was completed. Its location in Observatory Park in the Denver University Park neighborhood brings professional astronomers, members of the public, and amateur astronomers together to view stars, the moon, planets, nebulae, etc. The observatory still holds Public Nights and Open Houses to engage the local community with the mystery of space.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p style=\"color: #8b2332\"><b>The Chamberlin Observatory at the University of Denver, Colorado</b></p>\n\n<p>The Chamberlin Observatory at the University of Denver started observing Mars and other heavenly bodies in 1894. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=179
  BEFORE: "<p>The Upper Air Laboratory (UAL) was founded at the University of Colorado Boulder (CU Boulder) in 1948. It is now known as LASP. The laboratory was created a decade before NASA as a place to study Earth’s atmosphere and to launch suborbital rockets. Suborbital rockets are launched to the “edge” of outer space, but they do not have the energy to achieve orbit. When their engines turn off, they fall back to Earth due to gravity. In 1956, UAL solar rocket engineers partnered with the Ball Brothers Company and spun off to form the Ball Brothers Research Corporation, which is now Ball Aerospace & Technologies Corp. LASP continues to partner with Ball on numerous space missions today.</p>\n\n<p>The observations made by the ultraviolet spectrometer showed that carbon dioxide was the major constituent of the atmosphere and that the temperature of the upper atmosphere was 350 kelvin (K). Earth’s is between 700 K and 1200 K. Also, the escaping of atomic hydrogen removed most of Mars’ atmospheric water.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The Upper Air Laboratory (UAL) was founded at the University of Colorado Boulder (CU Boulder) in 1948. It is now known as LASP. The laboratory was created a decade before NASA as a place to study Earth’s atmosphere and to launch suborbital rockets. Suborbital rockets are launched to the “edge” of outer space, but they do not have the energy to achieve orbit. When their engines turn off, they fall back to Earth due to gravity. In 1956, UAL solar rocket engineers partnered with the Ball Brothers Company and spun off to form the Ball Brothers Research Corporation, which is now Ball Aerospace &amp; Technologies Corp. LASP continues to partner with Ball on numerous space missions today.</p>\n\n<p>The observations made by the ultraviolet spectrometer showed that carbon dioxide was the major constituent of the atmosphere and that the temperature of the upper atmosphere was 350 kelvin (K). Earth’s is between 700 K and 1200 K. Also, the escaping of atomic hydrogen removed most of Mars’ atmospheric water.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=180
  BEFORE: "<p>When Howe began teaching at DU, the university had no astronomy program or equipment. To initiate astronomical instruction, he borrowed a transit telescope from the U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey and installed it on campus. In 1881, he taught the university’s first astronomy course. Two years later, in 1883, Howe began doctoral work and oversaw the construction of a small observatory on the roof of the Junior Preparatory Building in downtown Denver.</p>\n\n<p> <a href=\"https://www.jstor.org/stable/1967399?searchText=%28ti%3A%28kepler%27s+problem%29+AND+au%3A%22howe%22%29&searchUri=%2Faction%2FdoAdvancedSearch%3Fq0%3Dkepler%2527s%2Bproblem%26q1%3Dhowe%26f0%3Dti%26c1%3DAND%26f1%3Dau%26acc%3Don%26so%3Drel&ab_segments=0%2Fbasic_phrase_search%2Fcontrol&refreqid=fastly-default%3A6cddb5b5059e0601e1bb801fe29fe691&seq=1\"><strong>Howe’s research</strong></a> focused on Kepler’s Problem, a classical mechanics question that forms a special case of the two-body problem. This problem considers how the positions and velocities of two massive bodies, influenced solely by their mutual gravitational attraction, evolve over time. In Kepler’s formulation, the interaction occurs through a central force that can vary in strength and be either attractive or repulsive. Solving Kepler’s Problem involves calculating the motion of these objects given their masses, positions, and velocities. In recognition of his research, particularly on Kepler’s Problem and his observational work on the Great Comet of 1882, Howe was awarded a Doctorate of Science from Boston University in June 1884.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>When Howe began teaching at DU, the university had no astronomy program or equipment. To initiate astronomical instruction, he borrowed a transit telescope from the U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey and installed it on campus. In 1881, he taught the university’s first astronomy course. Two years later, in 1883, Howe began doctoral work and oversaw the construction of a small observatory on the roof of the Junior Preparatory Building in downtown Denver.</p>\n\n<p> <a href=\"https://www.jstor.org/stable/1967399?searchText=%28ti%3A%28kepler%27s+problem%29+AND+au%3A%22howe%22%29&amp;searchUri=%2Faction%2FdoAdvancedSearch%3Fq0%3Dkepler%2527s%2Bproblem%26q1%3Dhowe%26f0%3Dti%26c1%3DAND%26f1%3Dau%26acc%3Don%26so%3Drel&amp;ab_segments=0%2Fbasic_phrase_search%2Fcontrol&amp;refreqid=fastly-default%3A6cddb5b5059e0601e1bb801fe29fe691&amp;seq=1\"><strong>Howe’s research</strong></a> focused on Kepler’s Problem, a classical mechanics question that forms a special case of the two-body problem. This problem considers how the positions and velocities of two massive bodies, influenced solely by their mutual gravitational attraction, evolve over time. In Kepler’s formulation, the interaction occurs through a central force that can vary in strength and be either attractive or repulsive. Solving Kepler’s Problem involves calculating the motion of these objects given their masses, positions, and velocities. In recognition of his research, particularly on Kepler’s Problem and his observational work on the Great Comet of 1882, Howe was awarded a Doctorate of Science from Boston University in June 1884.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=181
  BEFORE: "<p>As the University of Denver transitioned to its new and permanent campus in University Park, plans emerged for a more advanced observatory. On February 7, 1888, Chancellor David Hastings received a letter from Humphrey B. Chamberlin, a prominent Denver real estate developer, offering to finance the construction of a new observatory and all necessary equipment. Howe, the university’s most experienced astronomer, was asked to design the facility and select its primary instrument. He chose a 20-inch aperture refracting telescope built by Alvan Clark & Sons of Cambridge, Massachusetts, then one of the premier telescope makers in the United States.</p>\n\n<p>The observatory was constructed specifically to house this telescope. Its architecture includes reinforced floors to support the instrument’s substantial weight and a large iron dome with a movable shutter to allow unobstructed views of the night sky. The telescope arrived in 1894, and Howe oversaw its final assembly. Trial observations began on July 14 of that year, marking the official beginning of the Chamberlin Observatory’s scientific operations.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>As the University of Denver transitioned to its new and permanent campus in University Park, plans emerged for a more advanced observatory. On February 7, 1888, Chancellor David Hastings received a letter from Humphrey B. Chamberlin, a prominent Denver real estate developer, offering to finance the construction of a new observatory and all necessary equipment. Howe, the university’s most experienced astronomer, was asked to design the facility and select its primary instrument. He chose a 20-inch aperture refracting telescope built by Alvan Clark &amp; Sons of Cambridge, Massachusetts, then one of the premier telescope makers in the United States.</p>\n\n<p>The observatory was constructed specifically to house this telescope. Its architecture includes reinforced floors to support the instrument’s substantial weight and a large iron dome with a movable shutter to allow unobstructed views of the night sky. The telescope arrived in 1894, and Howe oversaw its final assembly. Trial observations began on July 14 of that year, marking the official beginning of the Chamberlin Observatory’s scientific operations.</p>"
