  BEFORE: "<p>The Upper Air Laboratory (UAL) was founded at the University of Colorado Boulder (CU Boulder) in 1948—more than a decade before NASA—in order to study Earth’s atmosphere and launch suborbital rockets. Suborbital rockets reach the “edge” of outer space but do not achieve orbit. When their engines shut off, they fall back to Earth due to gravity. <a href=\"https://www.colorado.edu/research/report/2021-22/75-years-innovation-space-research\"><strong>This year, CU Boulder celebrated 75 years of innovation in space research.</strong></a></p>\n\n<p>In 1956, UAL solar rocket engineers partnered with the Ball Brothers Company and spun off to form the Ball Brothers Research Corporation, which is now Ball Aerospace & Technologies Corp. UAL, now now as LASP, continues to collaborate with Ball on numerous space missions to this day.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The Upper Air Laboratory (UAL) was founded at the University of Colorado Boulder (CU Boulder) in 1948—more than a decade before NASA—in order to study Earth’s atmosphere and launch suborbital rockets. Suborbital rockets reach the “edge” of outer space but do not achieve orbit. When their engines shut off, they fall back to Earth due to gravity. <a href=\"https://www.colorado.edu/research/report/2021-22/75-years-innovation-space-research\"><strong>This year, CU Boulder celebrated 75 years of innovation in space research.</strong></a></p>\n\n<p>In 1956, UAL solar rocket engineers partnered with the Ball Brothers Company and spun off to form the Ball Brothers Research Corporation, which is now Ball Aerospace &amp; Technologies Corp. UAL, now now as LASP, continues to collaborate with Ball on numerous space missions to this day.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=191
  BEFORE: "<p>The lab was later renamed the <a href=\"”https://lasp.colorado.edu/\"><strong>Laboratory for Atmospheric and Space Physics (LASP)</strong></a>, and its expertise contributed significantly to early space exploration. The University of Colorado and LASP provided an ultraviolet spectrometer for NASA’s <a href=\"”\" https:=\"\" science.nasa.gov=\"\" mission=\"\" mariner-6=\"\"><strong>Mariner 6</strong></a> and <a href=\"”\" https:=\"\" science.nasa.gov=\"\" mission=\"\" mariner-7=\"\"><strong>Mariner 7</strong></a> missions, launched from Cape Kennedy in 1969, five years after the first successful flyby of Mars by Mariner 4, the only Mars mission with no Colorado institutional contributors.</p>\n\n<p>During the dual mission, Mariner 6 flew by Mars on July 31, 1969, at a distance of 3,431 km above the surface, and Mariner 7 followed six days later at a similar distance. Together, they captured 201 photos of the red planet. These missions were also the first to carry spectroscopic instruments, enabling new observations of the Martian atmosphere and surface.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The lab was later renamed the <a><strong>Laboratory for Atmospheric and Space Physics (LASP)</strong></a>, and its expertise contributed significantly to early space exploration. The University of Colorado and LASP provided an ultraviolet spectrometer for NASA’s <a><strong>Mariner 6</strong></a> and <a><strong>Mariner 7</strong></a> missions, launched from Cape Kennedy in 1969, five years after the first successful flyby of Mars by Mariner 4, the only Mars mission with no Colorado institutional contributors.</p>\n\n<p>During the dual mission, Mariner 6 flew by Mars on July 31, 1969, at a distance of 3,431 km above the surface, and Mariner 7 followed six days later at a similar distance. Together, they captured 201 photos of the red planet. These missions were also the first to carry spectroscopic instruments, enabling new observations of the Martian atmosphere and surface.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=192
  BEFORE: "<p>The ultraviolet spectrometer developed at LASP led to the discovery of ultraviolet airglow on Mars and made the first observations of atomic hydrogen escaping from the planet’s upper atmosphere. The data showed that carbon dioxide is the major component of the Martian atmosphere and that the temperature of the upper atmosphere is approximately 350 kelvin (K), compared to Earth's upper atmosphere, which ranges from 700 K to 1,200 K. The detection of escaping hydrogen also indicated that Mars had lost much of its atmospheric water.</p>\n\n<p>As of 2020, CU Boulder continues to play a key role in Mars exploration. <a href=\" https://www.colorado.edu/aerospace/jay-mcmahon\"> Assistant Professor of Engineering Jay McMahon</a> is researching and developing systems for future Mars missions. He has received a NASA Space Technology Research Early Career Faculty fellowship to support his work on Entry Guidance Methods for Precision Planetary Landers. While all successful Mars landings to date have carried relatively light payloads, McMahon is working to develop a pre-positioning system to help slow the descent to the Martian surface—an innovation that could support future manned missions to the Red Planet.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The ultraviolet spectrometer developed at LASP led to the discovery of ultraviolet airglow on Mars and made the first observations of atomic hydrogen escaping from the planet’s upper atmosphere. The data showed that carbon dioxide is the major component of the Martian atmosphere and that the temperature of the upper atmosphere is approximately 350 kelvin (K), compared to Earth's upper atmosphere, which ranges from 700 K to 1,200 K. The detection of escaping hydrogen also indicated that Mars had lost much of its atmospheric water.</p>\n\n<p>As of 2020, CU Boulder continues to play a key role in Mars exploration. <a href=\"https://www.colorado.edu/aerospace/jay-mcmahon\"> Assistant Professor of Engineering Jay McMahon</a> is researching and developing systems for future Mars missions. He has received a NASA Space Technology Research Early Career Faculty fellowship to support his work on Entry Guidance Methods for Precision Planetary Landers. While all successful Mars landings to date have carried relatively light payloads, McMahon is working to develop a pre-positioning system to help slow the descent to the Martian surface—an innovation that could support future manned missions to the Red Planet.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=193
  BEFORE: "<p style=\"color: #d83c06; font-size: 170%;\"><strong>WHAT IS THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN AN ORBITER, A LANDER, AND A ROVER?</strong></p>\n\n<p>Before examining the various contributions made by Colorado institutions to Mars exploration, it is important to first understand the distinct types of vehicles that have been developed to carry out these missions. <span style=\"color: #cb2314;\"><strong>Orbiters</strong></span>, <span style=\"color: #139aa1;\"><strong>landers</strong></span>, and <span style=\"color: #d83c06;\"><strong>rovers</strong></span> refer to the different types of vehicles used for Mars missions. <span style=\"color: #cb2314;\"><strong>Orbiters</strong></span> are spacecraft that make observations of the planet while they orbit at a nearby distance. <span style=\"color: #139aa1;\"><strong>Landers</strong></span> are the spacecraft that descend and come to a rest on the planet. <span style=\"color: #d83c06;\"><strong>Rovers</strong></span> are devices that explore the surface of the planet. While the other craft on Mars missions are spacecraft, <span style=\"color: #d83c06;\"><strong>rovers</strong></span> are designed as land vehicles. They makes their landing on a planet with the help of a <span style=\"color: #139aa1;\"><strong>lander</strong></span>.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p style=\"color: #8b2332\"><strong>WHAT IS THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN AN ORBITER, A LANDER, AND A ROVER?</strong></p>\n\n<p>Before examining the various contributions made by Colorado institutions to Mars exploration, it is important to first understand the distinct types of vehicles that have been developed to carry out these missions. <span style=\"color: #8b2332\"><strong>Orbiters</strong></span>, <span style=\"color: #139aa1\"><strong>landers</strong></span>, and <span style=\"color: #8b2332\"><strong>rovers</strong></span> refer to the different types of vehicles used for Mars missions. <span style=\"color: #8b2332\"><strong>Orbiters</strong></span> are spacecraft that make observations of the planet while they orbit at a nearby distance. <span style=\"color: #139aa1\"><strong>Landers</strong></span> are the spacecraft that descend and come to a rest on the planet. <span style=\"color: #8b2332\"><strong>Rovers</strong></span> are devices that explore the surface of the planet. While the other craft on Mars missions are spacecraft, <span style=\"color: #8b2332\"><strong>rovers</strong></span> are designed as land vehicles. They makes their landing on a planet with the help of a <span style=\"color: #139aa1\"><strong>lander</strong></span>.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=197
  BEFORE: "<p>Mariner 4 was the first successful mission to Mars, but there were no Colorado contributions. Mariner 4 completed the first \"fly-by\". This means that it passed Mars at a nearby distance and took a little under two dozen images of Mars' surface. These were the first photographs of the red planet at that proximity.</p>\n\n<a href=\"https://science.nasa.gov/mission/mariner-4/\" style=\"text-decoration: underline; color: #139aa1;\">\nVisit NASA's Mariner 4 mission website to learn more.\n</a>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Mariner 4 was the first successful mission to Mars, but there were no Colorado contributions. Mariner 4 completed the first \"fly-by\". This means that it passed Mars at a nearby distance and took a little under two dozen images of Mars' surface. These were the first photographs of the red planet at that proximity.</p>\n\n<a href=\"https://science.nasa.gov/mission/mariner-4/\">\nVisit NASA's Mariner 4 mission website to learn more.\n</a>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=199
  BEFORE: "<p>Mariner 6 & 7 were twin orbiters that made the first observations of atomic hydrogen escaping from the upper atmosphere of Mars. They also took 201 photographs of the Martian surface. On this mission, LASP at CU-Boulder provided the ultraviolet spectrometer that observed Mars' atmosphere for the first time.</p>\n\n<p>Ground observations of Mars were important to these early Mars missions, because until the first \"fly-bys\" the only images of Mars were captured through a telescope. NASA consulted with astronomers, such as Bradford A. Smith of the University of Arizona, to help them better understand the landscape of Mars to prepare for orbits and landings.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Mariner 6 &amp; 7 were twin orbiters that made the first observations of atomic hydrogen escaping from the upper atmosphere of Mars. They also took 201 photographs of the Martian surface. On this mission, LASP at CU-Boulder provided the ultraviolet spectrometer that observed Mars' atmosphere for the first time.</p>\n\n<p>Ground observations of Mars were important to these early Mars missions, because until the first \"fly-bys\" the only images of Mars were captured through a telescope. NASA consulted with astronomers, such as Bradford A. Smith of the University of Arizona, to help them better understand the landscape of Mars to prepare for orbits and landings.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><strong>$250,000 Cost of Re-Opening Opera House</br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192</br>\nJuly 10, 1932</strong></p>\n\n<p>$250,000 is over 10 times the amount spent to construct the building in 1878 (25 Things You Didn’t Know About Central City Opera, 2020), and would cost almost 6 million dollars today. Delos and Enda Chappell financed much of the restoration, and likely did not make their money back, with 7 performances and a ticket price of $2.75. The large investments put into the theater show the belief in success for the opera house’s future and the revival of the surrounding town.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</p></em>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=260
  BEFORE: "<P>Charles David Spivak was born in Russia in 1861 and died in Denver in 1927. He became a revolutionary Socialist in his youth and had to flee Russia to avoid being arrested by the Russian secret police. He arrived in New York City in 1882 and took various jobs until he learned English. He worked as a day laborer in New York, as a factory worker in Maine, and as a typesetter on the “Jewish Messenger.” In 1890, he graduated from Jefferson Medical College in Philadelphia with honors, and by 1885, he was Chief of Staff of the Philadelphia Polyclinics in Gastro-Internal diseases. In 1893, Charles Spivak married Jennie Charsky, and they moved to Denver in 1896 because of her poor health. The Spivak Family Papers and Art, B250, contains information on Charles and Jennie Spivak and their children.</p>\n\n<p>Dr. Spivak was a co-founder of the Jewish Consumptives’ Relief Society and was the executive secretary from 1904 until 1927. He was involved in every aspect of the JCRS and was affectionately known as “Papa Spivak.” Dr. Spivak, an ardent socialist, emphasized that the JCRS was to be a “people’s institution” and was proud of the fact that the institution often collected its funds in dollars and quarters from around the United States.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Charles David Spivak was born in Russia in 1861 and died in Denver in 1927. He became a revolutionary Socialist in his youth and had to flee Russia to avoid being arrested by the Russian secret police. He arrived in New York City in 1882 and took various jobs until he learned English. He worked as a day laborer in New York, as a factory worker in Maine, and as a typesetter on the “Jewish Messenger.” In 1890, he graduated from Jefferson Medical College in Philadelphia with honors, and by 1885, he was Chief of Staff of the Philadelphia Polyclinics in Gastro-Internal diseases. He was involved in every aspect of the JCRS and was affectionately known as “Papa Spivak.” Dr. Spivak, an ardent socialist, emphasized that the JCRS was to be a “people’s institution” and was proud of the fact that the institution often collected its funds in dollars and quarters from around the United States.</p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.description id=310
  BEFORE: "When City Lights Books published <em>Howl</em> in 1956, San Francisco police arrested publisher Lawrence Ferlinghetti and confiscated copies. The prosecution claimed the poem was obscene under community standards. At trial, expert witnesses testified to the poem’s cultural and artistic value. Judge Clayton Horn found that <em>HowlM/em> was not obscene and dismissed the charges. His decision reflected the Supreme Court’s new <em>Roth v. United States</em> standard, which required consideration of social importance. By clearing <em>Howl</em>, the court extended constitutional protection to avant-garde literature and set an early example of applying <em>Roth</em> to local censorship disputes."
  AFTER:  "When City Lights Books published <em>Howl</em> in 1956, San Francisco police arrested publisher Lawrence Ferlinghetti and confiscated copies. The prosecution claimed the poem was obscene under community standards. At trial, expert witnesses testified to the poem’s cultural and artistic value. Judge Clayton Horn found that <em>HowlM/em&gt; was not obscene and dismissed the charges. His decision reflected the Supreme Court’s new <em>Roth v. United States</em> standard, which required consideration of social importance. By clearing <em>Howl</em>, the court extended constitutional protection to avant-garde literature and set an early example of applying <em>Roth</em> to local censorship disputes.</em>"

  BEFORE: "<p><strong><em>Memoirs of a Woman of Pleasure (Fanny Hill)<em> by John Cleland</strong></p>\n\n<p>First published in London, 1748; Grove Press (New York, 1963 reprint)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Memoirs v. Massachusetts</em> (1966)<br>\n<strong>Status</strong>: Banned in Massachusetts until 1966</p>\n\n<p>John Cleland’s <em>Memoirs of a Woman of Pleasure</em>, better known as <em>Fanny Hill</em>, is an 18th-century erotic novel told from the perspective of a young woman reflecting on her sexual adventures. Long banned across Europe and the U.S., the book was notorious for explicit content but also recognized for its historical importance.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Censorship</strong><br>\nWhen Grove Press reissued <em>Fanny Hill</em> in 1963, Massachusetts officials sought to ban the book as obscene. State courts agreed, ruling that its sexual descriptions lacked redeeming value.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Court Decision</strong><br>\nIn <em>Memoirs v. Massachusetts</em> (1966), the U.S. Supreme Court reversed the state’s decision. The Court clarified that a work could only be obscene if it met all three conditions: appealed to prurient interest, was patently offensive, and was utterly without redeeming social value. Since <em>Fanny Hill</em> had at least some historical and literary merit, it could not be banned outright.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Why <em>Fanny Hill</em> Matters</strong><br>\nThe ruling significantly narrowed what could be legally defined as “obscene,” strengthening protections for literature and paving the way for wider publication of once-banned works. It also highlighted Grove Press’s role as a key defender of literary freedom in the 1960s.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Explore Further</strong><br>\n<a href=\"https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/383/413/\">Read the full Supreme Court opinion</a> | <a href=\"https://du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com/permalink/01UODE_INST/1shk5gq/alma991042008568002766\">Read online through the University of Denver Libraries</a></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong><em>Memoirs of a Woman of Pleasure (Fanny Hill)<em> by John Cleland</em></em></strong></p><em><em>\n\n<p>First published in London, 1748; Grove Press (New York, 1963 reprint)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Memoirs v. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=312
  BEFORE: "<p><strong><i>Catch-22</i> by Joseph Heller</strong></p>\n\n<p>Simon & Schuster (New York, 1961)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Minarcini v. Strongsville City School District</em> (1976)<br>\n<strong>Status</strong>: Banned in Strongsville, Ohio until reinstated</p>\n\n<p>Joseph Heller’s <em>Catch-22</em> is a biting satirical novel about World War II bomber pilots trapped in a bureaucratic paradox: to be grounded for insanity, one must request it, but making the request proves one’s sanity. Its absurd humor, critique of authority, and anti-war message made it a cultural touchstone of the 1960s and ’70s.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Censorship</strong><br>\nIn 1972, the Strongsville, Ohio school board removed <em>Catch-22</em> and Kurt Vonnegut’s <em>Cat’s Cradle</em> from libraries, citing “offensive language” and irreverence. Students challenged the decision, arguing that banning the books violated their First Amendment rights.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Court Decision</strong><br>\nIn 1976, the Sixth Circuit Court of Appeals ruled in <em>Minarcini v. Strongsville City School District</em> that removing books because of disagreement with their viewpoints was unconstitutional. The decision emphasized that school libraries exist as a forum for voluntary inquiry and cannot be narrowed to suit official preferences.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Why <em>Catch-22</em> Matters</strong><br>\nThe ruling reaffirmed that discomfort with controversial ideas is not sufficient grounds for censorship. <em>Catch-22</em>’s reinstatement reinforced the principle that satire, dissent, and irreverence deserve protection, especially in schools where exposure to diverse ideas is central to education.</p><strong>\n\n<p><strong>Explore Further</strong><br>\n<a href=\"https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp/384/698/1370485/\">Read the <em>Minarcini v. Strongsville</em> ruling</a> | <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EuRtPis9k0A\">Watch an interview with Joseph Heller</a> | <a href=\"https://du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com/permalink/01UODE_INST/1shk5gq/alma991041949596102766\">Read online as audiobook through the University of Denver Libraries</a></p></strong>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong><i>Catch-22</i> by Joseph Heller</strong></p>\n\n<p>Simon &amp; Schuster (New York, 1961)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Minarcini v. Strongsville City School District</em> (1976)<br>\n<strong>Status</strong>: Banned in Strongsville, Ohio until reinstated</p>\n\n<p>Joseph Heller’s <em>Catch-22</em> is a biting satirical novel about World War II bomber pilots trapped in a bureaucratic paradox: to be grounded for insanity, one must request it, but making the request proves one’s sanity. Students challenged the decision, arguing that banning the books violated their First Amendment rights.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Court Decision</strong><br>\nIn 1976, the Sixth Circuit Court of Appeals ruled in <em>Minarcini v. Strongsville City School District</em> that removing books because of disagreement with their viewpoints was unconstitutional. The decision emphasized that school libraries exist as a forum for voluntary inquiry and cannot be narrowed to suit official preferences.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Why <em>Catch-22</em> Matters</strong><br>\nThe ruling reaffirmed that discomfort with controversial ideas is not sufficient grounds for censorship. <em>Catch-22</em>’s reinstatement reinforced the principle that satire, dissent, and irreverence deserve protection, especially in schools where exposure to diverse ideas is central to education.</p><strong>\n\n<p><strong>Explore Further</strong><br>\n<a href=\"https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp/384/698/1370485/\">Read the <em>Minarcini v. Strongsville</em> ruling</a> | <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EuRtPis9k0A\">Watch an interview with Joseph Heller</a> | <a href=\"https://du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com/permalink/01UODE_INST/1shk5gq/alma991041949596102766\">Read online as audiobook through the University of Denver Libraries</a></p></strong>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=314
  BEFORE: "<p><strong><em>1984</em> by George Orwell</strong></p>\n\n<p>Secker & Warburg (London, 1949); Harcourt, Brace (New York, 1949)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Island Trees v. Pico</em> (1982) and later applications<br>\n<strong>Status</strong>: Challenged nationwide; removed in Florida (2022) but later reinstated</p>\n\n<p>George Orwell’s <em>1984</em> is a dystopian classic portraying a totalitarian state of surveillance, censorship, and propaganda. Its warnings about authoritarianism and control made it one of the most influential novels of the 20th century.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Censorship</strong><br>\nSince its release, <em>1984</em> has faced challenges across the political spectrum: during the Cold War, it was criticized as “pro-communist”; in later decades, it was removed for sexual content and strong language. In 2022, Duval County schools in Florida temporarily pulled it during a review of library collections.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Court Decision</strong><br>\nWhen defenders argued for its reinstatement, they cited <em>Island Trees v. Pico</em> (1982), in which the Supreme Court limited the removal of books for ideological reasons. The novel was returned to shelves after review.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Why <em>1984</em> Matters</strong><br>\nThe book itself is about censorship, making real-world attempts to suppress it particularly striking. <em>1984</em> remains central to debates about access to politically challenging literature, showing how legal precedents like <em>Pico</em> safeguard dissenting voices.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Explore Further</strong><br>\n<a href=\"https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/457/853/\">Read the <em>Island Trees v. Pico</em> ruling</a> | <a href=\"https://apnews.com/article/book-bans-education-florida-desantis-censorship-schools-b4647f7709d06ae7780dd03003dfcd90\">Learn about books pulled from shelves in Florida bans from the Associated Press</a> | <a href=\"https://du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com/permalink/01UODE_INST/1shk5gq/alma991042412286102766\">Read online through the University of Denver Libraries</a></p></strong>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong><em>1984</em> by George Orwell</strong></p>\n\n<p>Secker &amp; Warburg (London, 1949); Harcourt, Brace (New York, 1949)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Island Trees v. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=315
  BEFORE: "<p><strong><em>Fahrenheit 451</em> by Ray Bradbury</strong></p>\n\n<p>Ballantine Books (New York, 1953)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Island Trees v. Pico</em> (1982) and later applications<br>\n<strong>Status</strong>: Repeatedly challenged; reinstated in multiple districts</p>\n\n</p><p>Ray Bradbury’s <em>Fahrenheit 451</em> imagines a future where books are outlawed and “firemen” burn them. The novel critiques censorship, mass media, and societal apathy, making it one of the most enduring allegories about freedom of thought.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Censorship</strong><br>\nIronically, <em>Fahrenheit 451</em> itself has been frequently challenged in schools for offensive language, depictions of violence, and disturbing themes.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Court Decision</strong><br>\nDefenders often invoked <em>Island Trees v. Pico</em> (1982), where the Supreme Court held that school boards may not remove library books simply because they disagree with the ideas within them. Thanks to this precedent, <em>Fahrenheit 451</em> has been reinstated repeatedly after local challenges.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Why <em>Fahrenheit 451</em> Matters</strong><br>\nThe novel dramatizes the dangers of censorship, and its own contested history reinforces that warning. By remaining on library shelves, it continues to illustrate the enduring tension between suppression and intellectual freedom.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Explore Further</strong><br>\n<a href=\"https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/457/853/\">Read the <em>Island Trees v. Pico</em> ruling</a> | <a href=\"https://www.ala.org/bbooks/frequentlychallengedbooks/top10/archive\">Explore how Bradbury’s novel has been censored in the ALA’s Banned Books guide</a> | <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Pqp38_uS-eg\">Watch an Interview with Ray Bradbury</a> | <a href=\"https://du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com/permalink/01UODE_INST/1shk5gq/alma991042123097302766\">Read online through the University of Denver Libraries</a></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong><em>Fahrenheit 451</em> by Ray Bradbury</strong></p>\n\n<p>Ballantine Books (New York, 1953)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Island Trees v. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=319
  BEFORE: "<p><strong><em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> by the Boston Women’s Health Collective</strong></p>\n\n<p>New England Free Press (Boston, 1971); Simon & Schuster (New York, later editions)</p>\n\n<p><strong>Case</strong>: <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> (1987)<br>\n<strong>Status</strong>: Frequently challenged in the 1970s–1980s</p>\n\n<p>First published in 1971, <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> was a groundbreaking feminist health manual created by the Boston Women’s Health Collective. It offered frank, accessible information on women’s health, sexuality, and reproductive rights at a time when such topics were often taboo.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Censorship</strong><br>\nBecause of its explicit discussions and illustrations, the book was frequently targeted by schools, libraries, and conservative groups in the 1970s and 1980s. Critics labeled it obscene and sought to restrict access.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The Court Decision</strong><br>\nIn <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> (1987), the Supreme Court clarified how “serious literary, artistic, political, or scientific value” should be judged under the <em>Miller v. California</em> obscenity test. The Court held that value must be assessed by a reasonable person standard, not by local community views. This ensured that works like <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> could not be banned as obscene simply because they offended local sensibilities.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Why <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> Matters</strong><br>\nThe case strengthened protections for educational, scientific, and feminist literature, ensuring that communities could not impose narrow moral standards on works with recognized social value. The book itself became a touchstone of women’s empowerment and health advocacy.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Explore Further</strong><br>\n<a href=\"https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/481/497/\">Read the <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> opinion</a> | <a href=\"https://www.npr.org/2011/10/13/141320033/landmark-book-on-womens-sexuality-turns-40\">Learn about the impact of the book with NPR</a> | <a href=\"https://ourbodiesourselves.org/\"><em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> is no longer in print. Visit OurBodiesOurselves.org</a></p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.description id=319
  BEFORE: "<p>First published in 1971, <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> offered comprehensive information on women’s health and sexuality. Because of its explicit discussions and illustrations, it faced frequent challenges in schools and libraries. In <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> (1987), the Supreme Court refined the obscenity test. The Court held that a work’s “serious value” must be judged by a reasonable person standard rather than local community views. This ruling built on earlier cases such as <em>Memoirs v. Massachusetts</em> and strengthened protections for educational and scientific texts. The decision ensured that feminist health literature could not be dismissed as obscene.</P>"
  AFTER:  "<p>First published in 1971, <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> offered comprehensive information on women’s health and sexuality. Because of its explicit discussions and illustrations, it faced frequent challenges in schools and libraries. In <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> (1987), the Supreme Court refined the obscenity test. The Court held that a work’s “serious value” must be judged by a reasonable person standard rather than local community views. This ruling built on earlier cases such as <em>Memoirs v. Massachusetts</em> and strengthened protections for educational and scientific texts. The decision ensured that feminist health literature could not be dismissed as obscene.</p>"
--- tbl_standard_items.text id=325
  BEFORE: "<p>Cocina Libre is a movement that celebrates immigrant stories through food, creating spaces of belonging, dignity, and joy around the table. Founded on the belief that meals can connect people and amplify voices often left unheard, Cocina Libre brings communities together through books, documentaries, and shared meals that highlight cultural resilience. Since launching in 2024, the organization has reached #1 New Release status on Amazon, sold over 800 books, and helped immigrant chefs gain sustainable careers in the food industry. Its latest publication, <em>Sazón & Liberation</em>, is the second book written by Dr. Julia Roncoroni as part of Cocina Libre, further amplifying immigrant voices through story and recipe. Every event and purchase supports professional training, licensing, and income opportunities for immigrant chefs, turning meals into lasting impact.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Join the movement and learn more at <a href=\"https://cocinalibre.co/\">cocinalibre.co</a>.</strong></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Cocina Libre is a movement that celebrates immigrant stories through food, creating spaces of belonging, dignity, and joy around the table. Founded on the belief that meals can connect people and amplify voices often left unheard, Cocina Libre brings communities together through books, documentaries, and shared meals that highlight cultural resilience. Since launching in 2024, the organization has reached #1 New Release status on Amazon, sold over 800 books, and helped immigrant chefs gain sustainable careers in the food industry. Its latest publication, <em>Sazón &amp; Liberation</em>, is the second book written by Dr. Julia Roncoroni as part of Cocina Libre, further amplifying immigrant voices through story and recipe. Every event and purchase supports professional training, licensing, and income opportunities for immigrant chefs, turning meals into lasting impact.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Join the movement and learn more at <a href=\"https://cocinalibre.co/\">cocinalibre.co</a>.</strong></p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><em>Sazón & Liberation</em> presents food as a vessel of memory, resilience, and cultural identity. The stories shared here highlight the dignity inherent in every life and the power of shared meals to foster belonging and community across differences. This exhibit invites continued reflection on how culinary traditions serve as acts of resistance, preservation, and hope.</p>\n\n<p><strong>The exhibition is on physical display at the University of Denver in the Anderson Academic Commons, 2150 E Evans Ave, Denver, CO 80122, on the lower level from September 25, 2025 through January 11, 2026.</strong></p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=329
  BEFORE: "<b><em>Anatomia Botanica: A Book of Botanical Anatomies of the Sacred Lotus, Red Hibiscus and Southern Magnolia</em></b><br>\n<p>By Radha Pandey, 2014­</p>\n<p>Anatomia botanica was created for Radha Pandey’s graduate thesis at the University of Iowa Center for the Book. It also won the MICA Book Award at the Pyramid Atlantic Book Fair in 2014. Ms. Pandey is a papermaker and a letterpress printer. She is currently working on a book inspired by 17th-century Mughal floral portraiture and how it was impacted by colonialization. Her work was last shown in Denver in a group exhibit, Artists’ Book Cornucopia VI, at the Abecedarian Gallery.</p>\n\n<p>View the book in the DU Special Collections & Archives <b><u><a href=\"\" du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com=\"\" discovery=\"\" fulldisplay?context=\"L&vid=01UODE_INST:01UODE_MAIN&search_scope=MyInst_and_CI&tab=Everything&docid=alma991029784899702766\">here</a></u></b>.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<b><em>Anatomia Botanica: A Book of Botanical Anatomies of the Sacred Lotus, Red Hibiscus and Southern Magnolia</em></b><br>\n<p>By Radha Pandey, 2014­</p>\n<p>Anatomia botanica was created for Radha Pandey’s graduate thesis at the University of Iowa Center for the Book. It also won the MICA Book Award at the Pyramid Atlantic Book Fair in 2014. Ms. Pandey is a papermaker and a letterpress printer. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=330
  BEFORE: "<p>Anna Botsford Comstock was born in Cattaraugua County, New York, in 1854. She attended the newly opened Cornell University to study the natural sciences. At Cornell University, she met John Henry Comstock, an entomologist, and they later married and became partners. She illustrated many of his publications and co-authored A Manual for the Study of Insects in 1895.</p>\n\n<p>In 1897, Anna Botsford Comstock was hired as a professor of nature study at Cornell University. She was a part of the Nature Study Movement that sought to educate people in the natural sciences to encourage conservation. She continued to write and lecture after her retirement in 1922, and she passed away in 1930. Her contributions to the natural sciences and conservation were recognized in 1988 when the National Wildlife Federation named her the “Mother of Nature Education.”</p>\n\n<p><b></b></p><center><b>Below is a selection of books from DU Libraries' circulating collections.</b><p></p></center>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Anna Botsford Comstock was born in Cattaraugua County, New York, in 1854. She attended the newly opened Cornell University to study the natural sciences. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=339
  BEFORE: "<p>Mary Morris Vaux was born in 1860 in Philadelphia, PA. During annual family trips to the Canadian Rockies beginning in 1887, she became a mountain climber, outdoorswoman, photographer, and watercolor painter. In 1913, she climbed Mount Robson, the highest peak in the British Columbia Rockies, and in 1915 she married Charles Doolittle Walcott, a paleontologist and Secretary of the Smithsonian Institute. They met in the field while Walcott searched for invertebrate fossils in the Burgess Shale. Mary Vaux Walcott continued to travel to the Rocky Mountains every summer well into her old age.</p>\n<p>Between 1925 and 1928, Mary Vaux Walcott published four hundred drawings in five volumes of North American Wild Flowers. They were sold as part of her husband’s fund-raising campaign, and all proceeds went to the Smithsonian Institute’s endowment. Mount Mary Vaux in Jasper National Park in Alberta, Canada, was named in her honor.</p>\n<p><b></b></p><center><b>Below is a North American Wildflowers, Volume 1, and six prints from the volume from DU Libraries' Special Collections & Archives.</b><p></p></center>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Mary Morris Vaux was born in 1860 in Philadelphia, PA. --- tbl_standard_items.text id=347
  BEFORE: "<p>Maria Sibylla Merian was born in Frankfort am Main, a German territory, in 1647. After the death of her father, Matthaus Merian, an owner of printing and publishing houses, her mother married her second husband, Jacob Marrell, a Dutch still-life painter. He inspired Merian to become a skilled still-life artist specializing in flowers. </p><p>\n</p><p>In 1665, Maria Sibylla Merian married Johann Andreas Graff, an architectural painter. In 1691, she, her husband, and her daughters, Johanna Helena and Dorothea Maria, settled in Amsterdam, The Netherlands, where Meria could own her own business selling artist supplies, mounted insects, and preserved animals. In 1714, Merian suffered a stroke, and in 1717, she died.</p>\n<p>Maria Sibylla Merian is one of the most important contributors to the natural sciences. After she published her book Metamorphosis of the Insects of Suriname, her work became renowned in The Netherlands, England, Denmark, Germany, and France. Then, around twenty years after her death, Carl Linnaeus developed a new way to classify the natural world. In 1758, in the tenth edition of his Systema Naturae, he included around a hundred species using Maria Sibylla Merian’s illustrations.</p>\n<p></p><center><b>Below is a selection of books from DU Libraries' circulating collections. </b></center>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Maria Sibylla Merian was born in Frankfort am Main, a German territory, in 1647. After the death of her father, Matthaus Merian, an owner of printing and publishing houses, her mother married her second husband, Jacob Marrell, a Dutch still-life painter.   BEFORE: "<p><b><em>Marianne North: The Kew Collection</em></b><br>\nBy the Kew Royal Botanic Gardens, 2018</p>\n<p>The Marianne North Gallery in the Kew Royal Botanic Gardens first opened in 1882, and it featured 627 paintings of flowers and landscapes from thirteen countries. Her work has been shown in national and international juried exhibitions, such as the North American Print Biennial at the Danforth Museum in Framingham, Mass. Her last solo exhibition in Denver was “Look Again: Artist's Books and Prints by Deborah Bryan” at the Abecedarian Gallery in 2011.</p>\n\n<p>View the book in the DU Special Collections &amp; Archives <b><u><a> here</a></u></b>.</p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=402
  BEFORE: "<p><em>Denver Quarterly</em> embodies the University of Denver’s vision, values, and mission as DU’s only professional subscription-based literary arts journal dedicated to the public good. Founded in 1966, <em>Denver Quarterly</em> strives for excellence, innovation, engagement, integrity, and inclusiveness; our work is dedicated to advancing scholarly inquiry, cultivating critical and creative thought, and generating new knowledge in partnership with our writers and artists, educators and readers locally, nationally, and internationally.</p>\n\n<p><em>Denver Quarterly</em> is a vital space where innovation energizes tradition. The journal has had work honored in the <em>Pushcart Anthology, The PEN/O. Henry Prize Stories, The Best American Essays, The Best American Poetry,</em> and <em>The Best American Short Stories</em> among other celebrated collections. We are known for publishing works that limn the edges of experimentation and that play thoughtfully with convention. We seek to provide diverse publications that embody the wide-ranging possibilities for the worlds from which writing may come, and the worlds our authors imagine and write into being. We welcome the work of writers and artists from marginalized communities, identities, and traditions, or those who work with differing abilities, or who are non-professional writers, or who have otherwise been traditionally excluded from literary journals. That ethos may be found in our myriad pages since our journal’s first issue (Spring 1966). </p>\n\n<p><em>Denver Quarterly</em> is a professional literary arts journal with global reach that includes a diverse editorial community of graduate students, undergraduate students, and one dedicated faculty editor; the journal's year-round activities provide professional training in editing and publishing, summer internships, and partnerships with national and international literary arts organizations. Our subscriber list includes 400+ individuals and institutions nationally and internationally, and we receive approximately 5,000 submissions annually (sometimes more when we invite submissions for special topic folios). Each year, we publish and represent the works of approximately 150 artists/writers, which underscores the professionalism of our collaborative editorial reviewing process and the vitality of the works that we select for publication.</p>\n\n<p>Our journal has a rich legacy of publishing diverse forms of expression: poetry, prose, critical essays, interviews, translations, book reviews, visual artwork and photography. Since 2019, we have amplified our commitment to multicultural, international writing by including a greater number of bilingual texts in each of our volumes; we have also included a new modality of writing—performance—that highlights cross-genre, public-facing, and collaboratively written works, including play scripts and screen plays.</p>\n\n<p>We also publish between one and two issues of <em>FIVES</em> each year. <em>FIVES: a companion to Denver Quarterly</em> is our open-access electronic journal (dedicated to multimedia materials) that we launched in 2020. <em>FIVES</em> amplifies our journal’s commitment to representing works from emerging and established artists & writers at the intersections of innovative forms of expression and knowledge production. <em>FIVES</em> publishes new issues annually in addition to occasional DQ Online Folios that address special topics, such as “First Nations” (a collaborative project with <em>Westerly Magazine</em> (University of Western Australia), featuring works from Aboriginal, Indigenous, and Native artists and writers); and “From the Archives” (featuring research essays from the <em>Denver Quarterly</em> Records at the DU Library Archives).</p>\n\n<p>In addition to our publishing activities, we host public events (a fall poetry broadside workshop, and issue publication readings) as well as editorial training workshops and summer research internships. And we represent <em>Denver Quarterly</em> each year at the AWP convention (Association of Writers & Writing Programs), which provides essential professional development experience for our journal’s editors.</p><p></p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><em>Denver Quarterly</em> embodies the University of Denver’s vision, values, and mission as DU’s only professional subscription-based literary arts journal dedicated to the public good. Our subscriber list includes 400+ individuals and institutions nationally and internationally, and we receive approximately 5,000 submissions annually (sometimes more when we invite submissions for special topic folios). Each year, we publish and represent the works of approximately 150 artists/writers, which underscores the professionalism of our collaborative editorial reviewing process and the vitality of the works that we select for publication.</p>\n\n<p>Our journal has a rich legacy of publishing diverse forms of expression: poetry, prose, critical essays, interviews, translations, book reviews, visual artwork and photography. And we represent <em>Denver Quarterly</em> each year at the AWP convention (Association of Writers &amp; Writing Programs), which provides essential professional development experience for our journal’s editors.</p><p></p>"

--- tbl_standard_items.text id=414
  BEFORE: "<p></p><h1>Beacon Printing</h1><p></p>\n\n<p>Beacon Printing has been printing <em>Denver Quarterly</em> for decades, and we are deeply grateful for their outstanding work.</p>\n\n<p>Beacon Printing has been providing the Denver business community with quality commercial printing since 1960. With over 60 years of experience, their printing professionals are ready to provide quality, affordable commercial printing, pre-press, and bindery services.</p>\n<p>Beacon printing is committed to a sustainable environment and follows comprehensive environmental protocol, including the use of only Forest Stewardship Council (FSC) certified paper houses and recycled paper product options, a new varnish coating process that is environmentally friendly, soy-based inks, and recycling all all unused ink and all scrap paper. Unlike other local or national printers, Beacon Printing does not utilize a direct sales force. Instead, the owner and internal printing staff work individually with their clients, prioritizing higher savings, professionalism, and loyalty.</p>\n\n<p>Beacon Printing specializes in high-end, full-color printing. They enjoy working with their clients through the entire process, from state-of-the-art printing options to quality control. Their priority is to form long-term, one-to-one partnerships with their customers, built on mutual respect for work well done at reasonable and competitive prices.\n</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p></p><h2>Beacon Printing</h2><p></p>\n\n<p>Beacon Printing has been printing <em>Denver Quarterly</em> for decades, and we are deeply grateful for their outstanding work.</p>\n\n<p>Beacon Printing has been providing the Denver business community with quality commercial printing since 1960. With over 60 years of experience, their printing professionals are ready to provide quality, affordable commercial printing, pre-press, and bindery services.</p>\n<p>Beacon printing is committed to a sustainable environment and follows comprehensive environmental protocol, including the use of only Forest Stewardship Council (FSC) certified paper houses and recycled paper product options, a new varnish coating process that is environmentally friendly, soy-based inks, and recycling all all unused ink and all scrap paper. Unlike other local or national printers, Beacon Printing does not utilize a direct sales force. Instead, the owner and internal printing staff work individually with their clients, prioritizing higher savings, professionalism, and loyalty.</p>\n\n<p>Beacon Printing specializes in high-end, full-color printing. They enjoy working with their clients through the entire process, from state-of-the-art printing options to quality control. Their priority is to form long-term, one-to-one partnerships with their customers, built on mutual respect for work well done at reasonable and competitive prices.\n</p>"

--- tbl_grid_items.text id=3
  BEFORE: "<p>The Loewensteins' good friends, Dr. Ernst and Dr. Elisabeth Eylenburg, had arranged to send their fifteen-year-old daughter Erna to France in February 1939. Henry was to go with her on the train to Paris. However, the day they were to leave, Henry had come down with an illness and developed a high fever. His mother refused to let him travel as no one had a firm idea who would care for the children once they arrived in Paris.</p>\r\n<p>Erna (15-years-old) was sent to France on March 30, 1939. She later went to the Netherlands where she was apprehended by the Nazis, deported to Auschwitz, and murdered there in 1942.</p>\r\n<p>Both her parents and younger brother Walter were deported from Berlin to the Theresiennstadt concentration camp, which was in German occupied Czechoslovakia. On October 19, 1944, they were deported to Auschwitz where the entire family was killed.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>The Loewensteins' good friends, Dr. Ernst and Dr. Elisabeth Eylenburg, had arranged to send their fifteen-year-old daughter Erna to France in February 1939. Henry was to go with her on the train to Paris. However, the day they were to leave, Henry had come down with an illness and developed a high fever. His mother refused to let him travel as no one had a firm idea who would care for the children once they arrived in Paris.</p>\n<p>Erna (15-years-old) was sent to France on March 30, 1939. She later went to the Netherlands where she was apprehended by the Nazis, deported to Auschwitz, and murdered there in 1942.</p>\n<p>Both her parents and younger brother Walter were deported from Berlin to the Theresiennstadt concentration camp, which was in German occupied Czechoslovakia. On October 19, 1944, they were deported to Auschwitz where the entire family was killed.</p>"

--- tbl_grid_items.text id=20
  BEFORE: "<p>Henry’s beloved Uncle Georg Loewenstein (Max’s brother) and his wife Alice lived near the Loewensteins. During Kristallnacht (November 9-10, 1938), Georg, along with many other Jewish men, was taken to the Oranienburg Concentration Camp. Georg was wearing slippers, night clothes, and a robe when they came for him, yet the Nazis would not permit him to change or take a coat. When Georg was released in February 1939, his fingers and toes were badly frostbitten, and he was a broken man.</p>\r\n<p>During the Kristallnacht terror, assuming that the Nazis would not return a second time after taking Georg, the Loewensteins and distant family members hid in Uncle Georg’s apartment for a few weeks.</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p>Henry’s beloved Uncle Georg Loewenstein (Max’s brother) and his wife Alice lived near the Loewensteins. During Kristallnacht (November 9-10, 1938), Georg, along with many other Jewish men, was taken to the Oranienburg Concentration Camp. Georg was wearing slippers, night clothes, and a robe when they came for him, yet the Nazis would not permit him to change or take a coat. When Georg was released in February 1939, his fingers and toes were badly frostbitten, and he was a broken man.</p>\n<p>During the Kristallnacht terror, assuming that the Nazis would not return a second time after taking Georg, the Loewensteins and distant family members hid in Uncle Georg’s apartment for a few weeks.</p>"

The first two rows have been filled out.<br /></div>"

  BEFORE: "<div>Clothing ration card issued to Karin Steinberg. The front cover of the card is filled out by hand with Karin's name and address and stamped with the name and address of a shoemaker, \"Clemens Polley, Bülowstraße 3.\" The back cover is stamped with the date, June 19, 1940.   The inside of the card lists types of clothing and what they are worth. Around the edge are ration tabs some with Roman numerals and some with valid from dates. Most of these tabs are missing. The rest of the covers explain the legalities of the ration book, the assessment for substances, and the value of the goods.<br /></div>"

  BEFORE: "<div>Letter written by Marie Loewenstein in Berlin, Germany to Henry Loewenstein in Church Farm, Whipsnade, Dunstable, Beds, England through the Red Cross, dated by Marie February 25, 1944, stamped by an English post office March 11, 1944, and stamped received March 28, 1944. The front of this letter includes instructions in German and French for sending the letter, Marie's address, Henry's address, and a message about how the family is well and they miss Henry and long for a reunion signed by Max, Marie, and Karin. A Red Cross and Post Office stamp can be seen on the letter. The back of this letter is where the receiver could send a response however it is blank except for the printed directions for mailing in German and French.<br /></div>"

  BEFORE: "<p></p>\n<p></p>\n<p>The Yad Vashem Holocaust History Museum, located in Jerusalem, is Israel’s official memorial to the victims of the Holocaust. Dedicated to preserving the memory of the six million Jews murdered during the Holocaust, as well as the lives of Jewish communities destroyed, the museum offers a respectful and deeply moving experience. Through personal stories, original artifacts, photographs, and survivor testimonies, it presents a comprehensive and human-centered history of the Holocaust. Yad Vashem also serves as a center for education, research, and remembrance, fostering understanding and reflection on the consequences of hatred, antisemitism, and indifference.<br>\n<b>These four portraits are held in their collection.</b>"
  AFTER:  "<p></p>\n<p></p>\n<p>The Yad Vashem Holocaust History Museum, located in Jerusalem, is Israel’s official memorial to the victims of the Holocaust. Through personal stories, original artifacts, photographs, and survivor testimonies, it presents a comprehensive and human-centered history of the Holocaust. Yad Vashem also serves as a center for education, research, and remembrance, fostering understanding and reflection on the consequences of hatred, antisemitism, and indifference.<br>\n<b>These four portraits are held in their collection.</b></p>"

--- tbl_grid_items.text id=153
  BEFORE: "Spanish Folk-Poetry In New Mexico\nArthur Leon Campa\n1946\n\nWritten in 1946, the book Spanish Folk-Poetry in New Mexico analyzes the origins of traditional Latinx songs and poems from different time periods. Campa explains that the encomienda system and territorial conflicts pushed the Nahua to assimilate to new European cultures, and languages merged and were integrated into these regions (7-12). As a result, New Mexico is the first and only state to have bilingual laws (Campa 12). These songs mark the incorporations of Nahua and European influences in Latinx culture.\n\nCourtesy of University of Denver's Special Collections and Archives."

These songs mark the incorporations of Nahua and European influences in Latinx culture.</p><p>Courtesy of University of Denver's Special Collections and Archives.</p>"

These songs mark the incorporations of Nahua and European influences in Latinx culture.</p><p>Courtesy of University of Denver's Special Collections and Archives.</p>"

--- tbl_grid_items.description id=173
  BEFORE: "\"February, Tuesday 7. 1888.</br>\nSent my article on Kepler’s Problem off to Mr. Stone. In the evening Julian put a number of my books end to end, neatly, & compactly and then walked [illegible] on them, as on a walk. Right after chapel Dr. Moore amazed me by reading me a letter, which I did not look at, as it was confidential, and he did not tell me the man’s name, in which the writer declared it to be his intention to build and equip an observatory for us. The writer wanted Dr. Moore to send him plans for the building and instruments. So the Dr. asked me to work over the matter. I was quite excited, and worked pretty hard; but I could not satisfy myself about the building on account of the stair case. Dr. Moore said that if the writer’s plans succeeded, he would have the money.\""
  AFTER:  "\"February, Tuesday 7. 1888.<br>\nSent my article on Kepler’s Problem off to Mr. Stone. The writer wanted Dr. Moore to send him plans for the building and instruments. So the Dr. asked me to work over the matter. --- tbl_grid_items.description id=174
  BEFORE: "“Sat. July 14, 1894</br>\nSpent the forenoon in town, attending to various errands, while Mr. Hagan [Hagerd?] & Mr. Sprague worked some at the instrument. In the afternoon we worked on the refracting slow motion in ∝ (alpha), adjusting the rod in the polar axis, which gives slow motion in δ (delta), put on the eye-end and the objective. By working some in the evening the instrument was gotten fairly balanced and we looked through it. Fannie looked first at a star; after that the telescope was pointed to the moon and then to the clusters in Hercules, which looked fine despite moon & haze.”"
  AFTER:  "“Sat. July 14, 1894<br>\nSpent the forenoon in town, attending to various errands, while Mr. Hagan [Hagerd?] &amp; Mr. Sprague worked some at the instrument. In the afternoon we worked on the refracting slow motion in ∝ (alpha), adjusting the rod in the polar axis, which gives slow motion in δ (delta), put on the eye-end and the objective. By working some in the evening the instrument was gotten fairly balanced and we looked through it. Fannie looked first at a star; after that the telescope was pointed to the moon and then to the clusters in Hercules, which looked fine despite moon &amp; haze.”"
--- tbl_grid_items.description id=208
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>Transcript</strong><p>\n\n<p>\"Four, perhaps 64. Yeah, I think it was 1964. Bob Layton came to the lab to visit, with Clyde, and with me because they had a spacecraft headed toward Mars. They had a team that was to do an analysis of the Mars images and realized that they had nobody on their team that knew anything about Mars at all. That was the way things were done in those days. NASA essentially picked teams for their expertise in instrumentation, and so Leighton and Murray and other members of that group, including some geologists, were involved, but nobody who had any experience in looking at Mars from the Earth. And, Leighton came out to talk with us. And, he became very interested in the cumulative record that we had for Mars at that time in our photographic record and in Clyde's drawings. And, it was that point that we and the Mariner team and NASA itself began to realize the extreme importance of good ground-based records in tying together the very brief close-up views that a planetary probe is able to accomplish. And then tying that in what can be observed from the ground. By having essentially close-up views that you could then compare with the ground-based observations that were made at that same moment, then one might be able to infer things from the past and into the future.\"</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong>Transcript</strong></p><p>\n\n</p><p>\"Four, perhaps 64. Yeah, I think it was 1964. Bob Layton came to the lab to visit, with Clyde, and with me because they had a spacecraft headed toward Mars. They had a team that was to do an analysis of the Mars images and realized that they had nobody on their team that knew anything about Mars at all. That was the way things were done in those days. NASA essentially picked teams for their expertise in instrumentation, and so Leighton and Murray and other members of that group, including some geologists, were involved, but nobody who had any experience in looking at Mars from the Earth. And, Leighton came out to talk with us. And, he became very interested in the cumulative record that we had for Mars at that time in our photographic record and in Clyde's drawings. And, it was that point that we and the Mariner team and NASA itself began to realize the extreme importance of good ground-based records in tying together the very brief close-up views that a planetary probe is able to accomplish. And then tying that in what can be observed from the ground. By having essentially close-up views that you could then compare with the ground-based observations that were made at that same moment, then one might be able to infer things from the past and into the future.\"</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"Society Out to Recapture Spirit of Old Mining Town\"</strong></br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192</br>\nJuly 10, 1932</p>\n\n<p><em>“Every clique in Denver’s social realm is taking an active part in the project.”</em></p>\n\n<p>The hotels were sold out. Some were even buying and restoring houses in town. The acknowledgement of  history in Central City changed the economic structure of the town. Central City became dependent on tourism to their historical places, rather than the nearby town of Blackhawk, which turned to gambling, or Nevadaville, which eventually turned into a ghost town(Central City–Black Hawk Historic District | Colorado encyclopedia).</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</em></p>"

--- tbl_grid_items.text id=228
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"$250,000 Cost of Re-Opening Opera House\"</strong></br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192</br>\nJuly 10, 1932</p>\n\n<p>$250,000 is over 10 times the amount spent to construct the building in 1878 (25 Things You Didn’t Know About Central City Opera, 2020), and would cost almost 6 million dollars today. Delos and Enda Chappell financed much of the restoration, and likely did not make their money back, with 7 performances and a ticket price of $2.75. The large investments put into the theater show the belief in success for the opera house’s future and the revival of the surrounding town.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</p></em>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong>\"$250,000 Cost of Re-Opening Opera House\"</strong><br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192<br>\nJuly 10, 1932</p>\n\n<p>$250,000 is over 10 times the amount spent to construct the building in 1878 (25 Things You Didn’t Know About Central City Opera, 2020), and would cost almost 6 million dollars today.   BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"Central City Opens Bonanza Theater\"</strong></br>\nThe New York Times, Volume 81, Number 27, 203</br>\nJuly 16, 1932</p>\n\n<p>“Men, women and children from the Atlantic Seaboard and the Pacific Coast” sat in the 1878 original chairs, now engraved with dedications to the pioneers, paid for by their descendants to fund the restoration, including dedications to John Evans, William Gilpin, and John Gregory (<em>The Glory that was Gold</em>, 1932). There, in 1932, the past collided with the present, and the nation with Central City. With renewed attention, the once small mining town began to boom again.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy The New York Times</em></p>"

--- tbl_grid_items.text id=230
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"Woke Sleeping Theater</strong><br>\nAlberta Pike, The Rocky Mountain News, Volume 74, Number 21<br>\nAugust 6, 1933</strong></p>\n\n<p>“Wouldn’t it be marvelous if that lovely old theater, which had known such a fine tradition, could be made to live again!” said Ida Kruse McFarlane, who grew up in Central City. She emphasizes community support behind the reopening effort, with the opera originally providing happiness during economic prosperity, and later helping to revive the town economically. A year after reopening, many were already calling it an “American Salzburg,” the predominant summer opera in Europe.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</em></p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><strong>With a Bow to Miners</strong><br>\nJay Peake, The New York Times, Volume 119, Number 41,049<br>\nJune 14, 1970</p>\n\n<p>The annual traditions still drew crowds 40 years later. After the daily festivities ended, “not everyone stayed for the opera.” The festival had become about more than opera; it had become a celebration of the history of Central City and the revival effort. Central City became recognized as a National Historic Landmark in 1961 (Central City–Black Hawk Historic District | Colorado encyclopedia), and the opera house as a one in its own right, a few years after this article was written, in 1973 (Leyendecker & Eberhart, 1971).</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy The New York Times</em></p>"

  BEFORE: "\"[Cooking with my dad] was his way of teaching me. He is very closed person, very strict. It’s hard because he is never going to tell me, 'I love you, son.' Teaching me to cook was his way of showing me what he knew how to do, try to pass it on to me. At first, it was fun, learning, coming from school Friday, Saturday night; going to work; making a little bit of money. Along the way, he taught me a lot, but sometimes, he was really tough on me. Sometimes, I had to quit my soccer tournaments because he wanted me at the restaurant. At the time, as a teenager, I saw it as hard and personal. But now that I have my own kids, I’m grateful for it. He showed me how to work and be disciplined.\"</p>"
  AFTER:  "\"[Cooking with my dad] was his way of teaching me. He is very closed person, very strict. It’s hard because he is never going to tell me, 'I love you, son.' Teaching me to cook was his way of showing me what he knew how to do, try to pass it on to me. At first, it was fun, learning, coming from school Friday, Saturday night; going to work; making a little bit of money. Along the way, he taught me a lot, but sometimes, he was really tough on me. Sometimes, I had to quit my soccer tournaments because he wanted me at the restaurant. At the time, as a teenager, I saw it as hard and personal. But now that I have my own kids, I’m grateful for it. He showed me how to work and be disciplined.\"<p></p>"
--- tbl_grid_items.description id=342
  BEFORE: "“Conozco a una señora y le cuento mi situación. Me dice: ‘Sabes qué, Erika, aquí hay una iglesia, hay una institución donde dan comida gratis.’ Me llevó, me inscribió, me dio la mano. Con la comida que ellos me habían regalado, yo de agradecimiento, preparé un buffet en mi casa y lo llevé. Les encantó. Me dijeron: ‘Sabes qué, vamos a hacer otro evento y te vamos a contratar para que puedas vender tu comida y todos puedan conocer tu comida.’ Me motiva el querer hacer conocer mis raíces. Estaba un poco preocupada porque no sabía dónde encontrar los condimentos y los productos peruanos. Hasta que en los mercados mexicanos encontré algunos productos. Entonces dije: ‘No. Yo tengo que cocinar mi comida y que la gente la conozca.’ El desafío me motivó a que las personas prueben mi comida con mi cultura. También empecé a fusionar con la comida mexicana, con la americana. Y es lo que me motiva, que la gente sepa que se pueden hacer muchas cosas con la comida, con mis raíces, con mis condimentos y con los productos peruanos que están aquí en Estados Unidos.” English Translation: I met a woman and told her about my situation. She said, “You know what, Erika, there’s a church here, an organization where they give out free food.” She took me there, registered me, and gave me a hand. With the food they had given me, out of gratitude I prepared a buffet at home and brought it over. They loved it. They said, “You know what, we’re going to do another event and we’re going to hire you so you can sell your food and everyone can get to know your food.”\nI’m motivated by wanting to make my roots known. I was a little worried because I didn’t know where to find Peruvian seasonings and products—until I found some items at Mexican markets. Then I said, “No. I have to cook my food so people can get to know it.” The challenge motivated me to have people try my food with my culture. I also started fusing it with Mexican food and with American food. And that’s what motivates me—that people know there’s so much you can do with food, with my roots, with my seasonings, and with the Peruvian products that are here in the United States."
  AFTER:  "<p>“Conozco a una señora y le cuento mi situación. Me dice: ‘Sabes qué, Erika, aquí hay una iglesia, hay una institución donde dan comida gratis.’ Me llevó, me inscribió, me dio la mano. Con la comida que ellos me habían regalado, yo de agradecimiento, preparé un buffet en mi casa y lo llevé. Les encantó. Me dijeron: ‘Sabes qué, vamos a hacer otro evento y te vamos a contratar para que puedas vender tu comida y todos puedan conocer tu comida.’ Me motiva el querer hacer conocer mis raíces. Estaba un poco preocupada porque no sabía dónde encontrar los condimentos y los productos peruanos. Hasta que en los mercados mexicanos encontré algunos productos. Entonces dije: ‘No. Yo tengo que cocinar mi comida y que la gente la conozca.’ El desafío me motivó a que las personas prueben mi comida con mi cultura. También empecé a fusionar con la comida mexicana, con la americana. Y es lo que me motiva, que la gente sepa que se pueden hacer muchas cosas con la comida, con mis raíces, con mis condimentos y con los productos peruanos que están aquí en Estados Unidos.” English Translation: I met a woman and told her about my situation. She said, “You know what, Erika, there’s a church here, an organization where they give out free food.” She took me there, registered me, and gave me a hand. With the food they had given me, out of gratitude I prepared a buffet at home and brought it over. They loved it. They said, “You know what, we’re going to do another event and we’re going to hire you so you can sell your food and everyone can get to know your food.”<br>I’m motivated by wanting to make my roots known. I was a little worried because I didn’t know where to find Peruvian seasonings and products—until I found some items at Mexican markets. Then I said, “No. I have to cook my food so people can get to know it.” The challenge motivated me to have people try my food with my culture. I also started fusing it with Mexican food and with American food. And that’s what motivates me—that people know there’s so much you can do with food, with my roots, with my seasonings, and with the Peruvian products that are here in the United States.</p>"
--- tbl_grid_items.description id=343
  BEFORE: "“Quiero que la gente pruebe y decir: ‘Ah, me recuerda a cuando estaba en Monterrey y comía eso.’  Tengo un postre, el Carlota de limón, por ejemplo. Era el postre que cuando estaba en la prepa y hacíamos fiestas, había como cinco versiones del mismo postre. Como los brownies, haz de cuenta. Todo el mundo hacía brownies. Todo el mundo hacía Carlota. Y me ha pasado, cuando tenía mi restaurante de cocina mexicana, hace varios años, gente de Monterrey que iba porque escuchaban que el chef era de Monterrey. Y aprovechaban, ‘pues, déjame ir a ver.’ Probaban la barbacoa, probaban el carlota, y decían: ‘Es que transporté a cuando estaba en Monterrey.’ Y para mí, esa es la frase que estoy buscando: que la gente diga ‘me transportaste a México.’ No quiere decir que todos los platillos van a ser exitosos desde esa perspectiva, pero esa es la meta. Que alguien pueda probar algo y decir: ‘Ah, sí, sabe como cuando estuve en Guanajuato y probé esto,’ o ‘sabe como cuando estuve en Oaxaca y probé un mole…’ Eso, para mí, es la meta: que la gente viaje a través de mi comida de la misma manera que yo he viajado a través de la comida.” English Translation: I want people to taste it and say, “Ah, this reminds me of when I was in Monterrey and ate this.” I have a dessert—Carlota de limón, for example. Back in high school, whenever we had parties, there were like five versions of the same dessert. Like brownies, you know. Everyone made brownies. Everyone made Carlota.\nAnd it’s happened that, when I had my Mexican restaurant years ago, people from Monterrey would come because they’d heard the chef was from Monterrey. They’d think, “Well, let me go check it out.” They’d try the barbacoa, they’d try the carlota, and they’d say, “It transported me back to when I was in Monterrey.” And for me, that’s the phrase I’m looking for: for people to say, “You transported me to Mexico.” It doesn’t mean every dish will be successful from that perspective, but that’s the goal: that someone can taste something and say, “Ah, yes, it tastes like when I was in Guanajuato and I tried this,” or “it tastes like when I was in Oaxaca and I tried a mole…” That, for me, is the goal: that people travel through my food in the same way I have traveled through food."
  AFTER:  "<p>“Quiero que la gente pruebe y decir: ‘Ah, me recuerda a cuando estaba en Monterrey y comía eso.’  Tengo un postre, el Carlota de limón, por ejemplo. Era el postre que cuando estaba en la prepa y hacíamos fiestas, había como cinco versiones del mismo postre. Como los brownies, haz de cuenta. Todo el mundo hacía brownies. Todo el mundo hacía Carlota. Y me ha pasado, cuando tenía mi restaurante de cocina mexicana, hace varios años, gente de Monterrey que iba porque escuchaban que el chef era de Monterrey. Y aprovechaban, ‘pues, déjame ir a ver.’ Probaban la barbacoa, probaban el carlota, y decían: ‘Es que transporté a cuando estaba en Monterrey.’ Y para mí, esa es la frase que estoy buscando: que la gente diga ‘me transportaste a México.’ No quiere decir que todos los platillos van a ser exitosos desde esa perspectiva, pero esa es la meta. Que alguien pueda probar algo y decir: ‘Ah, sí, sabe como cuando estuve en Guanajuato y probé esto,’ o ‘sabe como cuando estuve en Oaxaca y probé un mole…’ Eso, para mí, es la meta: que la gente viaje a través de mi comida de la misma manera que yo he viajado a través de la comida.” English Translation: I want people to taste it and say, “Ah, this reminds me of when I was in Monterrey and ate this.” I have a dessert—Carlota de limón, for example. Back in high school, whenever we had parties, there were like five versions of the same dessert. Like brownies, you know. Everyone made brownies. Everyone made Carlota.<br>And it’s happened that, when I had my Mexican restaurant years ago, people from Monterrey would come because they’d heard the chef was from Monterrey. They’d think, “Well, let me go check it out.” They’d try the barbacoa, they’d try the carlota, and they’d say, “It transported me back to when I was in Monterrey.” And for me, that’s the phrase I’m looking for: for people to say, “You transported me to Mexico.” It doesn’t mean every dish will be successful from that perspective, but that’s the goal: that someone can taste something and say, “Ah, yes, it tastes like when I was in Guanajuato and I tried this,” or “it tastes like when I was in Oaxaca and I tried a mole…” That, for me, is the goal: that people travel through my food in the same way I have traveled through food.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<p><b><em>Marianne North: The Kew Collection</em></b><br>\nBy the Kew Royal Botanic Gardens, 2018</p>\n<p>The Marianne North Gallery in the Kew Royal Botanic Gardens first opened in 1882, and it featured 627 paintings of flowers and landscapes from thirteen countries. Between 1833 and 1885, that gallery was extended to accommodate the paintings made during North’s last three journeys to South Africa, Seychelles, and Chile. The gallery then held 848 artworks. All of these paintings are still on display and can be seen today. This book is the first time that the entire collection has been published.</p>\n<p>View the book in the DU Libraries <b><u><a href=\"\" du.primo.exlibrisgroup.com=\"\" discovery=\"\" fulldisplay?context=\"L&vid=01UODE_INST:01UODE_MAIN&search_scope=MyInst_and_CI&tab=Everything&docid=alma991041832197902766\">here</a>.</u></b></p>"

  BEFORE: "In the 1950s the Glenn L. Martin company completed its new Titan missile plant near Denver, Colorado. In 1955, Martin-Marietta, originally the Glenn L. Martin Company and now Lockheed Martin, was established in Jefferson County near Waterton. The Titan missile was used to launch Viking 1 &amp; 2, Mars Pathfinder, Mars Odyssey, and Spirit and Opportunity of the Mars Exploration Plan. Titan flew on its last mission in 2005. Lockheed Martin is still working to build new technologies that will one day reach Mars."
