--- tbl_grids.text id=41
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;The United Launch Alliance (ULA), formed in 2006, is a spacecraft launch service provider that is a joint venture between Lockheed Martin and Boeing that works with NASA, the Department of Defense, and other organizations. In 1969, Mariner 6 &amp; 7 were launched on the first Atlas rocket, developed by Convair, a spacecraft manufacturing company founded in 1943 in San Diego, California. An Atlas rocket was then again used in the launches of Mariner 9, Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter, Mars Science Laboratory mission with the Curiosity rover, MAVEN, and InSignt missions. Other rockets developed by and then acquired by ULA in 2006 were the Titan rocket the launched the Viking orbiters and landers, and Mars Pathfinder, Mars Odyssey, and Spirit and Opportunity of the Mars Exploration Plan, and the Delta rockets that launched the Pheonix lander.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;In March 2016, ULA experienced controversy. The senior engineer at ULA, Brett Tobey, resigned on March 16th, 2016, after he made controversial comments about the US government improperly favoring ULA over SpaceX when awarding contracts for launches during a presentation at the University of Colorado Boulder. The Pentagon has since launched investigations into the allegations.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>The United Launch Alliance (ULA), formed in 2006, is a spacecraft launch service provider that is a joint venture between Lockheed Martin and Boeing that works with NASA, the Department of Defense, and other organizations. In 1969, Mariner 6 &amp; 7 were launched on the first Atlas rocket, developed by Convair, a spacecraft manufacturing company founded in 1943 in San Diego, California. An Atlas rocket was then again used in the launches of Mariner 9, Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter, Mars Science Laboratory mission with the Curiosity rover, MAVEN, and InSignt missions. Other rockets developed by and then acquired by ULA in 2006 were the Titan rocket the launched the Viking orbiters and landers, and Mars Pathfinder, Mars Odyssey, and Spirit and Opportunity of the Mars Exploration Plan, and the Delta rockets that launched the Pheonix lander.</p>\n\n<p>In March 2016, ULA experienced controversy. The senior engineer at ULA, Brett Tobey, resigned on March 16th, 2016, after he made controversial comments about the US government improperly favoring ULA over SpaceX when awarding contracts for launches during a presentation at the University of Colorado Boulder. The Pentagon has since launched investigations into the allegations.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=42
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Ball Aerospace &amp; Technologies Corp., now part of BAE Systems, was originally founded through a collaboration between University of Colorado Boulder’s UAL solar rocket engineers and the Colorado-based Ball Brothers Company. Today, one of its notable contributions to space exploration is the High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment (HiRISE) camera aboard NASA’s Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter. Developed in partnership with the University of Arizona, HiRISE is the largest telescopic camera ever deployed beyond Earth’s orbit. It captures images at five times the resolution of those from the earlier Mars Global Surveyor, enabling researchers to study the Martian surface in unprecedented detail and aiding in the selection of landing sites for both robotic and future human missions.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Ball Aerospace &amp; Technologies Corp., now part of BAE Systems, was originally founded through a collaboration between University of Colorado Boulder’s UAL solar rocket engineers and the Colorado-based Ball Brothers Company. Today, one of its notable contributions to space exploration is the High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment (HiRISE) camera aboard NASA’s Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter. Developed in partnership with the University of Arizona, HiRISE is the largest telescopic camera ever deployed beyond Earth’s orbit. It captures images at five times the resolution of those from the earlier Mars Global Surveyor, enabling researchers to study the Martian surface in unprecedented detail and aiding in the selection of landing sites for both robotic and future human missions.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=45
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Between 1962 and 1973, NASA’s Mariner program sent a series of small robotic spacecraft to explore Venus, Mars, and Mercury. Among them, &lt;strong&gt;Mariners 3–9&lt;&#x2F;strong&gt; transformed our understanding of Mars. In 1965, &lt;strong&gt;Mariner 4&lt;&#x2F;strong&gt; captured the first close-up images of another planet, revealing a cratered, moon-like surface. &lt;strong&gt;Mariners 6 and 7&lt;&#x2F;strong&gt; followed in 1969 with detailed flybys, and in 1971, &lt;strong&gt;Mariner 9&lt;&#x2F;strong&gt; became the first spacecraft to orbit another planet. After waiting out a massive dust storm, it mapped 100% of Mars’ surface, uncovering towering volcanoes, vast canyons, and ancient riverbeds, the evidence that Mars was once more Earth-like than imagined.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Though built by NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in California, the Mariner missions had key support from Colorado. Scientists at the University of Colorado Boulder’s Laboratory for Atmospheric and Space Physics (LASP) helped interpret atmospheric data, while Ball Aerospace in Boulder contributed to the development of space-based optical instruments. These early contributions helped launch Colorado’s lasting role in planetary exploration.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;em&gt;Learn more about the &lt;a ref:&quot;https:=&quot;&quot; science.nasa.gov=&quot;&quot; mission=&quot;&quot; mariner-program=&quot;&quot; mars-mariner-missions=&quot;&quot; &quot;=&quot;&quot;&gt;Mariner missions to Mars on NASA’s website.&lt;&#x2F;a&gt;&lt;&#x2F;em&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Between 1962 and 1973, NASA’s Mariner program sent a series of small robotic spacecraft to explore Venus, Mars, and Mercury. Among them, <strong>Mariners 3–9</strong> transformed our understanding of Mars. In 1965, <strong>Mariner 4</strong> captured the first close-up images of another planet, revealing a cratered, moon-like surface. <strong>Mariners 6 and 7</strong> followed in 1969 with detailed flybys, and in 1971, <strong>Mariner 9</strong> became the first spacecraft to orbit another planet. After waiting out a massive dust storm, it mapped 100% of Mars’ surface, uncovering towering volcanoes, vast canyons, and ancient riverbeds, the evidence that Mars was once more Earth-like than imagined.</p>\n\n<p>Though built by NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in California, the Mariner missions had key support from Colorado. Scientists at the University of Colorado Boulder’s Laboratory for Atmospheric and Space Physics (LASP) helped interpret atmospheric data, while Ball Aerospace in Boulder contributed to the development of space-based optical instruments. These early contributions helped launch Colorado’s lasting role in planetary exploration.</p>\n\n<em>Learn more about the <a>Mariner missions to Mars on NASA’s website.</a></em>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=47
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Lockheed Martin was chosen by NASA to be the principal industrial contractor of the Viking Project, which launched in 1975. The project included two orbiters that would study Mars from above and two landers that would study the surface. Martin Marietta, later Lockheed Martin, designed and built the two landers, as well as the rockets used to launch the Viking mission from Earth.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;A few weeks after the successful landing of the first Viking lander, Viking 2 landed on Mars. Together, the landers took soil samples and took pictures, then sent the data back to Earth. Each orbiter and lander operated far longer than their designed lifetime of 90 days. Viking Lander 1 made its final transmission on November 11, 1982.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Lockheed Martin was chosen by NASA to be the principal industrial contractor of the Viking Project, which launched in 1975. The project included two orbiters that would study Mars from above and two landers that would study the surface. Martin Marietta, later Lockheed Martin, designed and built the two landers, as well as the rockets used to launch the Viking mission from Earth.</p>\n\n<p>A few weeks after the successful landing of the first Viking lander, Viking 2 landed on Mars. Together, the landers took soil samples and took pictures, then sent the data back to Earth. Each orbiter and lander operated far longer than their designed lifetime of 90 days. Viking Lander 1 made its final transmission on November 11, 1982.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=53
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;The early German Jews in Denver had integrated well into the general population, and many had achieved economic, political, and social success. Because of the onslaught of consumptives, beds for patients in city and state general hospitals were so scarce that the poor were frequently left to their own devices, often dying in the streets. Frances Wisebart Jacobs launched a relentless campaign to raise public awareness on behalf of indigent consumptives, seeking the support of businessmen and political leaders to fund a new hospital. Her efforts were initially rebuffed by city leaders. Then she turned to the Jewish community, which rallied to establish a sanatorium.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Denver’s Jewish Hospital Organization was formally incorporated in 1890 and was to be managed and funded by the Jewish community, but it would have a nonsectarian admissions policy. Jacobs found an ally in a young, energetic rabbi who had just come to Denver: William Sterne Friedman. Rabbi Friedman enlisted the financial support of some of the trustees of his congregation, Temple Emanuel, insisting that concern for the sick and indigent had always been a vital tenet of Jewish tradition. The hospital was initially named the Frances Jacobs Hospital. Frances Wisebart Jacobs Clothing and Papers, B148 provides more information on Frances Jacobs and the National Jewish.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>The early German Jews in Denver had integrated well into the general population, and many had achieved economic, political, and social success. Because of the onslaught of consumptives, beds for patients in city and state general hospitals were so scarce that the poor were frequently left to their own devices, often dying in the streets. Frances Wisebart Jacobs launched a relentless campaign to raise public awareness on behalf of indigent consumptives, seeking the support of businessmen and political leaders to fund a new hospital. Her efforts were initially rebuffed by city leaders. Then she turned to the Jewish community, which rallied to establish a sanatorium.</p>\n\n<p>Denver’s Jewish Hospital Organization was formally incorporated in 1890 and was to be managed and funded by the Jewish community, but it would have a nonsectarian admissions policy. Jacobs found an ally in a young, energetic rabbi who had just come to Denver: William Sterne Friedman. Rabbi Friedman enlisted the financial support of some of the trustees of his congregation, Temple Emanuel, insisting that concern for the sick and indigent had always been a vital tenet of Jewish tradition. The hospital was initially named the Frances Jacobs Hospital. Frances Wisebart Jacobs Clothing and Papers, B148 provides more information on Frances Jacobs and the National Jewish.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=54
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Many of Denver’s early pioneer physicians became closely associated with National Jewish Hospital (NJH). Dr. John Elsner, who founded Denver’s first hospital and was a proponent of the founding of National Jewish, became a member of the first NJH Medical Advisory Board, along with his nephew, Dr. Robert Levy. They were joined by Dr. Saling Simon, Dr. M. Kleiner, and Dr. S.R. Feil.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;The first medical director at NJH, Dr. Moses Collins, was followed by Saling Simon, who migrated to Denver in search of a cure for his own tuberculosis. Dr. Simon, who served as the medical director of National Jewish from 1917 to 1919, instituted the hospital’s research department and served as a trustee of the hospital for over fifty years. His son, Walter Simon, later served as president of NJH from 1959 to 1962.  John Elsner, M.D. Collection, B186 and Robert Levy Papers, B372 have additional information on these doctors.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Many of Denver’s early pioneer physicians became closely associated with National Jewish Hospital (NJH). They were joined by Dr. Saling Simon, Dr. M. Kleiner, and Dr. S.R. Feil.</p>\n\n<p>The first medical director at NJH, Dr. Moses Collins, was followed by Saling Simon, who migrated to Denver in search of a cure for his own tuberculosis. --- tbl_grids.text id=55
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Through the efforts of Seraphine Pisko, the Hofheimer Preventorium was built and opened in 1920. The Preventorium was a place where healthy but impoverished children could receive medically supervised, nutritious food, exercise, and love. Both Christmas and Chanukah were celebrated.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;The establishment of the Hofheimer Preventorium created a need for regularized instruction. Mayme Smith Schoenwald, who began working at National Jewish in 1920, established an accredited education program for children at the hospital. Classes at National Jewish continued through the eighth grade and followed the Denver Public Schools syllabus. When Mayme Smith Schoenwald stopped teaching in 1944, Anne Slager Niblock took over the school. The Denver Public School continued when National Jewish began treating asthma and other respiratory diseases. The hospital evolved into the National Jewish Medical and Research Center and became the number one respiratory hospital in the United States. The oral history interviews of both Mayme Smith Schoenwald and Anne Slager Niblock are in the Rocky Mountain Jewish Historical Society Oral Histories, B098 in the Beck Archives.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Through the efforts of Seraphine Pisko, the Hofheimer Preventorium was built and opened in 1920. The Preventorium was a place where healthy but impoverished children could receive medically supervised, nutritious food, exercise, and love. Both Christmas and Chanukah were celebrated.</p>\n\n<p>The establishment of the Hofheimer Preventorium created a need for regularized instruction. Mayme Smith Schoenwald, who began working at National Jewish in 1920, established an accredited education program for children at the hospital. Classes at National Jewish continued through the eighth grade and followed the Denver Public Schools syllabus. When Mayme Smith Schoenwald stopped teaching in 1944, Anne Slager Niblock took over the school. The Denver Public School continued when National Jewish began treating asthma and other respiratory diseases. The hospital evolved into the National Jewish Medical and Research Center and became the number one respiratory hospital in the United States. The oral history interviews of both Mayme Smith Schoenwald and Anne Slager Niblock are in the Rocky Mountain Jewish Historical Society Oral Histories, B098 in the Beck Archives.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=57
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Dr. Philip Hillkowitz was born in Lithuania in 1873 and died in Denver in 1948. He was president of the Jewish Consumptives’ Relief Society from its beginning in 1904 until his death in 1948. His father, Rabbi Elias Hillkowitz, who suffered from asthma, brought the family to Denver in 1890. Rabbi Hillkowitz suggested the JCRS motto from the Talmud: &#x27;&#x27;He who saves one life is considered as if he had preserved the whole world.&#x27;&#x27; His daughter, Anna Hillkowitz Bresler, took leave from her job as a Denver librarian to work as a field secretary for the JCRS. She traveled around the United States raising money for the Sanatorium beginning in 1906.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Dr. Hillkowitz was determined to make the JCRS a comfortable environment for indigent Jewish immigrant consumptives and often affirmed his goal of not allowing the JCRS to become “a cold, complexionless institution, which just happens to be inhabited by Hebrews.” In addition to kosher ethnic food, the JCRS held regular celebrations of the Jewish Sabbath and holidays, as well as making it a haven for Jews who held radical socialist political views. Ben and Bessie Glass were patients at the JCRS and talked about life “at the San” in an oral history.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Dr. Philip Hillkowitz was born in Lithuania in 1873 and died in Denver in 1948. He was president of the Jewish Consumptives’ Relief Society from its beginning in 1904 until his death in 1948. His father, Rabbi Elias Hillkowitz, who suffered from asthma, brought the family to Denver in 1890. Rabbi Hillkowitz suggested the JCRS motto from the Talmud: ''He who saves one life is considered as if he had preserved the whole world.'' His daughter, Anna Hillkowitz Bresler, took leave from her job as a Denver librarian to work as a field secretary for the JCRS. She traveled around the United States raising money for the Sanatorium beginning in 1906.</p>\n\n<p>Dr. Hillkowitz was determined to make the JCRS a comfortable environment for indigent Jewish immigrant consumptives and often affirmed his goal of not allowing the JCRS to become “a cold, complexionless institution, which just happens to be inhabited by Hebrews.” In addition to kosher ethnic food, the JCRS held regular celebrations of the Jewish Sabbath and holidays, as well as making it a haven for Jews who held radical socialist political views. Ben and Bessie Glass were patients at the JCRS and talked about life “at the San” in an oral history.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=58
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Abraham (Avram) Judelovitz was born in Mitau, Russia, on April 14, 1986, and died in Denver in 1948. He married Fannie Rose Baer on June 15, 1886, in Lithuania-Russia. Avram and Fannie Judelovitz arrived in New York in July 1886. Avram was a contractor, builder, and worked in real estate. He co-founded the Jewish Consumptives&#x27; Relief Society and the Ex-Patients Tubercular Home. Abraham Judelovitz helped construct more permanent buildings to house and treat patients at the JCRS. The Judd Family Papers, B088, have construction papers from three generations of builders.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Louis Robinson, another immigrant from Eastern Europe, donated the land on which the JCRS dairy farm was created, providing patients with fresh milk and eggs daily. Robinson and his family founded the successful Robinson Dairy business, and his descendants continued to take an active role in Jewish communal affairs for five generations. Upon Louis Robinson’s death in 1923, the JCRS trustees issued a special resolution to honor his many contributions: “The JCRS was fortunate enough to acquire his individual service, his constant attention, his whole-souled devotion over a long period of years, and under his ministrations, the Sanatorium of the Jewish Consumptives’ Relief Society literally became verdant and bloomed and fructified.” The Robinson Family Papers, B289.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Abraham (Avram) Judelovitz was born in Mitau, Russia, on April 14, 1986, and died in Denver in 1948. He married Fannie Rose Baer on June 15, 1886, in Lithuania-Russia. --- tbl_grids.text id=60
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;For many years, the relationship between National Jewish Hospital and the Jewish Consumptives’ Relief Society was acrimonious, with leaders disagreeing about medical issues, and religious and administrative policies. As time passed, the relationship between the two hospitals became more amiable, and the two institutions spearheaded the founding of two other pivotal local Denver Jewish institutions, the Denver Sheltering Home for Jewish Children and the Ex-Patients’ Home. Eventually, the Sheltering Home morphed into the National Asthma Center and then merged into National Jewish Hospital. The National Asthma Center Records, B098, contain photographs and records from the opening of the Denver Sheltering Home for Jewish Children in 1907, through its merger with National Jewish Hospital in 1978.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;If German and East Europeans sometimes clashed over the work of the two tuberculosis hospitals, they were able to unite when the cause was dependent children. The Sheltering Home, while representative of Jewish orphanages in general, was founded in reaction to a rather unusual set of circumstances: what was to become of Jewish children who were neglected, abandoned, orphaned, or half-orphaned due to their parents’ tuberculosis. Rabbi Charles Kauvar, spiritual leader of the traditional Beth Ha Medrosh Hagodol (BMH), served as one of the first vice presidents of the Home, along with Rabbi Friedman of the reform Congregation Emanuel.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>For many years, the relationship between National Jewish Hospital and the Jewish Consumptives’ Relief Society was acrimonious, with leaders disagreeing about medical issues, and religious and administrative policies. As time passed, the relationship between the two hospitals became more amiable, and the two institutions spearheaded the founding of two other pivotal local Denver Jewish institutions, the Denver Sheltering Home for Jewish Children and the Ex-Patients’ Home. Eventually, the Sheltering Home morphed into the National Asthma Center and then merged into National Jewish Hospital. The National Asthma Center Records, B098, contain photographs and records from the opening of the Denver Sheltering Home for Jewish Children in 1907, through its merger with National Jewish Hospital in 1978.</p>\n\n<p>If German and East Europeans sometimes clashed over the work of the two tuberculosis hospitals, they were able to unite when the cause was dependent children. The Sheltering Home, while representative of Jewish orphanages in general, was founded in reaction to a rather unusual set of circumstances: what was to become of Jewish children who were neglected, abandoned, orphaned, or half-orphaned due to their parents’ tuberculosis. Rabbi Charles Kauvar, spiritual leader of the traditional Beth Ha Medrosh Hagodol (BMH), served as one of the first vice presidents of the Home, along with Rabbi Friedman of the reform Congregation Emanuel.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=61
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Fannie Eller Lorber (1881-1958) was a popular choice for president of the Sheltering Home, an office she would hold until her death in 1958. Fannie Lorber often bridged the divide between the German and Russian Jews. Money for the institution was collected by a band of dedicated volunteers, and Mrs. Lorber and Mrs. Willens often traveled the city together in a horse and buggy. Mrs. Willens drove while Fannie, who spoke better English, made the “pitch” to American-born contributors. As one Sheltering Home administrator later recalled, “Whatever Fannie wanted, Fannie got…she was a very dynamic person, even though she spoke with a heavy Yiddish accent.” For many years, Bessie Willens proudly proclaimed, “I made Mrs. Lorber for president.” The Fannie Eller Lorber Papers, B272, illustrate the life of a remarkable woman.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Mrs. Lorber, whose business and organizational talents became legendary, helped create a network of women’s auxiliaries that boasted over 100,000 members nationally at the time of her death. She often pointed out that “the cause of the child is the cause of humanity. In the tradition of Judaism, the cause of the underprivileged child has always been placed at the head of every charity and philanthropy.”&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Fannie Eller Lorber (1881-1958) was a popular choice for president of the Sheltering Home, an office she would hold until her death in 1958. Fannie Lorber often bridged the divide between the German and Russian Jews. Money for the institution was collected by a band of dedicated volunteers, and Mrs. Lorber and Mrs. Willens often traveled the city together in a horse and buggy. Mrs. Willens drove while Fannie, who spoke better English, made the “pitch” to American-born contributors. As one Sheltering Home administrator later recalled, “Whatever Fannie wanted, Fannie got…she was a very dynamic person, even though she spoke with a heavy Yiddish accent.” For many years, Bessie Willens proudly proclaimed, “I made Mrs. Lorber for president.” The Fannie Eller Lorber Papers, B272, illustrate the life of a remarkable woman.</p>\n\n<p>Mrs. Lorber, whose business and organizational talents became legendary, helped create a network of women’s auxiliaries that boasted over 100,000 members nationally at the time of her death. She often pointed out that “the cause of the child is the cause of humanity. In the tradition of Judaism, the cause of the underprivileged child has always been placed at the head of every charity and philanthropy.”</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=62
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;The Home originally sheltered only orphaned or neglected children of tuberculosis victims, but it soon expanded its mission to help combat delinquency among Denver’s Jewish children. An arrangement was made with Judge Ben B. Lindsey to send first-time offenders to the home rather than to a detention center. (Judge Lindsey created the Colorado Juvenile Court system.) The Home also began taking in orphaned and needy children from other cities. The aim of the Home’s founders was to provide a cultured, stimulating, and Jewish environment for the children, as close to family life as possible.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;The Home expanded and became a national organization in the 1920s with an office in New York City in addition to the office in Denver. A cottage plan was adopted, where children were housed in small group homes to recreate a more family-like structure. The Sheltering Home provided not only food and shelter, but high-level educational resources. According to William Cohen, the longtime superintendent, although the children followed tightly regulated schedules with a host of extra-curricular activities, were assigned chores, and were encouraged to develop good study habits, “from its inception the Home was characterized by the absence of institutionalism of any kind.”&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>The Home originally sheltered only orphaned or neglected children of tuberculosis victims, but it soon expanded its mission to help combat delinquency among Denver’s Jewish children. An arrangement was made with Judge Ben B. Lindsey to send first-time offenders to the home rather than to a detention center. (Judge Lindsey created the Colorado Juvenile Court system.) The Home also began taking in orphaned and needy children from other cities. The aim of the Home’s founders was to provide a cultured, stimulating, and Jewish environment for the children, as close to family life as possible.</p>\n\n<p>The Home expanded and became a national organization in the 1920s with an office in New York City in addition to the office in Denver. A cottage plan was adopted, where children were housed in small group homes to recreate a more family-like structure. The Sheltering Home provided not only food and shelter, but high-level educational resources. According to William Cohen, the longtime superintendent, although the children followed tightly regulated schedules with a host of extra-curricular activities, were assigned chores, and were encouraged to develop good study habits, “from its inception the Home was characterized by the absence of institutionalism of any kind.”</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=63
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;In 1939, the Home instituted the long-term residential treatment of children with intractable asthma. In the 1950s, medical, psychiatric, and research personnel were added to treat children with asthma and allergies. In 1951, Dr. Allan Hurst became the first full-time medical director, and Jack Gershtenson became the administrator, a position he held for nearly thirty years. The Home officially became non-sectarian in 1953. Dr. Murray Pershkin, chief consultant for the Home from 1940 until 1959, advocated “parentectomy,” the removal of the child from his or her home for up to two years. The practice was not popular among some of the children.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;In 1957, the renowned Children’s Asthma Research Institute and Hospital (CARIH) was created. At the time, it was the only research facility in the country dedicated to the study of asthma and allergic diseases. In 1966, Drs. Kimishige and Taruko Ishizake, a husband and wife research team at CARIH, discovered immunoglobulin E, the physiological basis for asthma. In 1972, the Sheltering Home became the National Asthma Center (NAC). National Jewish Hospital had evolved from treating tuberculosis to addressing a broader range of respiratory diseases, including asthma. It was decided in 1978 that a merger of the two institutions would be beneficial. Patients remained at the NAC campus until 1981, when it was closed.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>In 1939, the Home instituted the long-term residential treatment of children with intractable asthma. In the 1950s, medical, psychiatric, and research personnel were added to treat children with asthma and allergies. In 1951, Dr. Allan Hurst became the first full-time medical director, and Jack Gershtenson became the administrator, a position he held for nearly thirty years. The Home officially became non-sectarian in 1953. Dr. Murray Pershkin, chief consultant for the Home from 1940 until 1959, advocated “parentectomy,” the removal of the child from his or her home for up to two years. The practice was not popular among some of the children.</p>\n\n<p>In 1957, the renowned Children’s Asthma Research Institute and Hospital (CARIH) was created. At the time, it was the only research facility in the country dedicated to the study of asthma and allergic diseases. In 1966, Drs. Kimishige and Taruko Ishizake, a husband and wife research team at CARIH, discovered immunoglobulin E, the physiological basis for asthma. In 1972, the Sheltering Home became the National Asthma Center (NAC). National Jewish Hospital had evolved from treating tuberculosis to addressing a broader range of respiratory diseases, including asthma. It was decided in 1978 that a merger of the two institutions would be beneficial. Patients remained at the NAC campus until 1981, when it was closed.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=65
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;Many prominent Denver Jewish leaders such as Simon Quiat (1887-1981), Max Schradsky (1872-1933), Moses “Morris” Binstock (1885-1968), and John Streltzer (1901-1985) served as presidents of the Ex-Patients’ Home. The Quiatkowsky family and Max Schradsky were part of the failed agricultural community in Cotopaxi, Colorado, but later moved to Denver. Morris Binstock, born in Poland, was another successful businessman and humanitarian. The Collection on Cotopaxi Colony, B321; Binstock Papers, B287; and oral histories with Simon Quiat and John E. Streltzer have more information on these men.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Philanthropist John E. Streltzer was born in Denver’s West Side. He served nearly three terms as a member of the Colorado House of Representatives in the 1950s. During his third term, President John F. Kennedy appointed Streltzer Director of Customs for Colorado and Wyoming, a position he held until 1969. Throughout his life, he was involved in many Jewish, general, and medical causes, including Ex-Patients’ Home, Israel Bonds, B&#x27;nai B&#x27;rith, AMC Cancer Research Center (JCRS), Red Cross, United Way, and Allied Jewish Welfare Fund. The John E. Streltzer Papers, B185, provides more information on John Streltzer.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p>Many prominent Denver Jewish leaders such as Simon Quiat (1887-1981), Max Schradsky (1872-1933), Moses “Morris” Binstock (1885-1968), and John Streltzer (1901-1985) served as presidents of the Ex-Patients’ Home. The Quiatkowsky family and Max Schradsky were part of the failed agricultural community in Cotopaxi, Colorado, but later moved to Denver. Morris Binstock, born in Poland, was another successful businessman and humanitarian. The Collection on Cotopaxi Colony, B321; Binstock Papers, B287; and oral histories with Simon Quiat and John E. Streltzer have more information on these men.</p>\n\n<p>Philanthropist John E. Streltzer was born in Denver’s West Side. He served nearly three terms as a member of the Colorado House of Representatives in the 1950s. During his third term, President John F. Kennedy appointed Streltzer Director of Customs for Colorado and Wyoming, a position he held until 1969. Throughout his life, he was involved in many Jewish, general, and medical causes, including Ex-Patients’ Home, Israel Bonds, B'nai B'rith, AMC Cancer Research Center (JCRS), Red Cross, United Way, and Allied Jewish Welfare Fund. The John E. Streltzer Papers, B185, provides more information on John Streltzer.</p>"
--- tbl_grids.text id=67
  BEFORE: "The 1980s and early 1990s marks the rise of the AIDS&#x2F;HIV epidemic which was infamously attributed to gay men and injection-drug users. The delay in response to the crisis led to thousands of deaths of Americans, neglected the care they needed from the government and society (Santos, 2019). To memorialize those who lost their lives to AIDS, gay activists like Cleve Jones started the AIDS Quilt and formed the Names Project Foundation (&quot;The History of the Quilt&quot;, n.d.). DU displayed a part of the quilt in 1992 (Hubanks, 1992), bringing attention to the LGBTQ+ community who were disproportionately affected by the disease."
  AFTER:  "The 1980s and early 1990s marks the rise of the AIDS/HIV epidemic which was infamously attributed to gay men and injection-drug users. The delay in response to the crisis led to thousands of deaths of Americans, neglected the care they needed from the government and society (Santos, 2019). To memorialize those who lost their lives to AIDS, gay activists like Cleve Jones started the AIDS Quilt and formed the Names Project Foundation (\"The History of the Quilt\", n.d.). --- tbl_grids.text id=105
  BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;&lt;h1&gt;Alice Walker&lt;&#x2F;h1&gt;&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;Alice Walker is the author of the Pulitzer Prize-winning novel, &lt;em&gt;The Color Purple&lt;&#x2F;em&gt; (1982); she was the first African-American woman to receive the award. In addition to being a prolific and critically lauded novelist, short story writer and poet, Walker is renowned for her outspoken activism; she has made meaningful contributions to second-wave feminism, civil rights, and peace movements. In 1983 Walker coined the term ‘womanism’ to address the specific oppressions faced by Black feminists and women of color. She contributed a short story, ‘The Child Who Favored Daughter’ to volume 2 issue 2 of &lt;em&gt;Denver Quarterly&lt;&#x2F;em&gt;, published in Summer 1967. This was subsequently reprinted in the journal’s 30th anniversary issue 31.&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p></p><h2>Alice Walker</h2><p></p>\n\n<p>Alice Walker is the author of the Pulitzer Prize-winning novel, <em>The Color Purple</em> (1982); she was the first African-American woman to receive the award. In addition to being a prolific and critically lauded novelist, short story writer and poet, Walker is renowned for her outspoken activism; she has made meaningful contributions to second-wave feminism, civil rights, and peace movements. In 1983 Walker coined the term ‘womanism’ to address the specific oppressions faced by Black feminists and women of color. She contributed a short story, ‘The Child Who Favored Daughter’ to volume 2 issue 2 of <em>Denver Quarterly</em>, published in Summer 1967.   BEFORE: "&lt;p&gt;&lt;h1&gt;Audre Lorde&lt;&#x2F;h1&gt;&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;\n\n&lt;p&gt;As a poet, intersectional feminist, professor, writer, and activist Audre Lorde made vital contributions to both literature and social theory. As a self-described “Black, lesbian, feminist, socialist, mother, warrior, poet” with notable writings emerging during the 1970s and 1980s, Lorde’s identity and lived experiences informed her work. Lorde’s significant books, &lt;em&gt;Sister Outsider: Essays and Speeches and The Cancer&lt;&#x2F;em&gt; Journals, describe her experiences and incredible advocacy work. Lorde submitted several poems to &lt;em&gt;Denver Quarterly&lt;&#x2F;em&gt; for publication, such as “Contact Lenses,” “Chorus,” and “Between Ourselves.” Her poem “Evening News” was published for the first time in Denver Quarterly (1983).&lt;&#x2F;p&gt;"
  AFTER:  "<p></p><h2>Audre Lorde</h2><p></p>\n\n<p>As a poet, intersectional feminist, professor, writer, and activist Audre Lorde made vital contributions to both literature and social theory. As a self-described “Black, lesbian, feminist, socialist, mother, warrior, poet” with notable writings emerging during the 1970s and 1980s, Lorde’s identity and lived experiences informed her work. Lorde’s significant books, <em>Sister Outsider: Essays and Speeches and The Cancer</em> Journals, describe her experiences and incredible advocacy work. --- tbl_media_library.description id=285
  BEFORE: "When City Lights Books published <em>Howl</em> in 1956, San Francisco police arrested publisher Lawrence Ferlinghetti and confiscated copies. The prosecution claimed the poem was obscene under community standards. At trial, expert witnesses testified to the poem’s cultural and artistic value. Judge Clayton Horn found that <em>HowlM/em> was not obscene and dismissed the charges. His decision reflected the Supreme Court’s new <em>Roth v. United States</em> standard, which required consideration of social importance. By clearing <em>Howl</em>, the court extended constitutional protection to avant-garde literature and set an early example of applying <em>Roth</em> to local censorship disputes."
  AFTER:  "When City Lights Books published <em>Howl</em> in 1956, San Francisco police arrested publisher Lawrence Ferlinghetti and confiscated copies. The prosecution claimed the poem was obscene under community standards. At trial, expert witnesses testified to the poem’s cultural and artistic value. Judge Clayton Horn found that <em>HowlM/em&gt; was not obscene and dismissed the charges. His decision reflected the Supreme Court’s new <em>Roth v. United States</em> standard, which required consideration of social importance. By clearing <em>Howl</em>, the court extended constitutional protection to avant-garde literature and set an early example of applying <em>Roth</em> to local censorship disputes.</em>"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=294
  BEFORE: "<p>First published in 1971, <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> offered comprehensive information on women’s health and sexuality. Because of its explicit discussions and illustrations, it faced frequent challenges in schools and libraries. In <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> (1987), the Supreme Court refined the obscenity test. The Court held that a work’s “serious value” must be judged by a reasonable person standard rather than local community views. This ruling built on earlier cases such as <em>Memoirs v. Massachusetts</em> and strengthened protections for educational and scientific texts. The decision ensured that feminist health literature could not be dismissed as obscene.</P>"
  AFTER:  "<p>First published in 1971, <em>Our Bodies, Ourselves</em> offered comprehensive information on women’s health and sexuality. Because of its explicit discussions and illustrations, it faced frequent challenges in schools and libraries. In <em>Pope v. Illinois</em> (1987), the Supreme Court refined the obscenity test. The Court held that a work’s “serious value” must be judged by a reasonable person standard rather than local community views. This ruling built on earlier cases such as <em>Memoirs v. Massachusetts</em> and strengthened protections for educational and scientific texts. The decision ensured that feminist health literature could not be dismissed as obscene.</p>"

  BEFORE: "<div>Letter written by Marie Loewenstein in Berlin, Germany to Henry Loewenstein in Church Farm, Whipsnade, Dunstable, Beds, England through the Red Cross, dated by Marie February 25, 1944, stamped by an English post office March 11, 1944, and stamped received March 28, 1944. The front of this letter includes instructions in German and French for sending the letter, Marie's address, Henry's address, and a message about how the family is well and they miss Henry and long for a reunion signed by Max, Marie, and Karin. The back of this letter is where the receiver could send a response however it is blank except for the printed directions for mailing in German and French.<br /></div>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=574
  BEFORE: "\"February, Tuesday 7. 1888.</br>\nSent my article on Kepler’s Problem off to Mr. Stone. In the evening Julian put a number of my books end to end, neatly, & compactly and then walked [illegible] on them, as on a walk. Right after chapel Dr. Moore amazed me by reading me a letter, which I did not look at, as it was confidential, and he did not tell me the man’s name, in which the writer declared it to be his intention to build and equip an observatory for us. The writer wanted Dr. Moore to send him plans for the building and instruments. So the Dr. asked me to work over the matter. I was quite excited, and worked pretty hard; but I could not satisfy myself about the building on account of the stair case. Dr. Moore said that if the writer’s plans succeeded, he would have the money.\""
  AFTER:  "\"February, Tuesday 7. 1888.<br>\nSent my article on Kepler’s Problem off to Mr. Stone. --- tbl_media_library.description id=576
  BEFORE: "“Sat. July 14, 1894</br>\nSpent the forenoon in town, attending to various errands, while Mr. Hagan [Hagerd?] & Mr. Sprague worked some at the instrument. In the afternoon we worked on the refracting slow motion in ∝ (alpha), adjusting the rod in the polar axis, which gives slow motion in δ (delta), put on the eye-end and the objective. By working some in the evening the instrument was gotten fairly balanced and we looked through it. Fannie looked first at a star; after that the telescope was pointed to the moon and then to the clusters in Hercules, which looked fine despite moon & haze.”"
  AFTER:  "“Sat. July 14, 1894<br>\nSpent the forenoon in town, attending to various errands, while Mr. Hagan [Hagerd?] &amp; Mr. Sprague worked some at the instrument. In the afternoon we worked on the refracting slow motion in ∝ (alpha), adjusting the rod in the polar axis, which gives slow motion in δ (delta), put on the eye-end and the objective. By working some in the evening the instrument was gotten fairly balanced and we looked through it. Fannie looked first at a star; after that the telescope was pointed to the moon and then to the clusters in Hercules, which looked fine despite moon &amp; haze.”"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=611
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>Transcript</strong><p>\n\n<p>\"Four, perhaps 64. Yeah, I think it was 1964. Bob Layton came to the lab to visit, with Clyde, and with me because they had a spacecraft headed toward Mars. They had a team that was to do an analysis of the Mars images and realized that they had nobody on their team that knew anything about Mars at all. That was the way things were done in those days. NASA essentially picked teams for their expertise in instrumentation, and so Leighton and Murray and other members of that group, including some geologists, were involved, but nobody who had any experience in looking at Mars from the Earth. And, Leighton came out to talk with us. And, he became very interested in the cumulative record that we had for Mars at that time in our photographic record and in Clyde's drawings. And, it was that point that we and the Mariner team and NASA itself began to realize the extreme importance of good ground-based records in tying together the very brief close-up views that a planetary probe is able to accomplish. And then tying that in what can be observed from the ground. By having essentially close-up views that you could then compare with the ground-based observations that were made at that same moment, then one might be able to infer things from the past and into the future.\"</p>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong>Transcript</strong></p><p>\n\n</p><p>\"Four, perhaps 64. Yeah, I think it was 1964. Bob Layton came to the lab to visit, with Clyde, and with me because they had a spacecraft headed toward Mars. They had a team that was to do an analysis of the Mars images and realized that they had nobody on their team that knew anything about Mars at all. That was the way things were done in those days. NASA essentially picked teams for their expertise in instrumentation, and so Leighton and Murray and other members of that group, including some geologists, were involved, but nobody who had any experience in looking at Mars from the Earth. And, Leighton came out to talk with us. And, he became very interested in the cumulative record that we had for Mars at that time in our photographic record and in Clyde's drawings. And, it was that point that we and the Mariner team and NASA itself began to realize the extreme importance of good ground-based records in tying together the very brief close-up views that a planetary probe is able to accomplish. And then tying that in what can be observed from the ground. By having essentially close-up views that you could then compare with the ground-based observations that were made at that same moment, then one might be able to infer things from the past and into the future.\"</p>"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=628
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"Society Out to Recapture Spirit of Old Mining Town\"</strong></br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192</br>\nJuly 10, 1932</p>\n\n<p><em>“Every clique in Denver’s social realm is taking an active part in the project.”</em></p>\n\n<p>The hotels were sold out. Some were even buying and restoring houses in town. The acknowledgement of  history in Central City changed the economic structure of the town. Central City became dependent on tourism to their historical places, rather than the nearby town of Blackhawk, which turned to gambling, or Nevadaville, which eventually turned into a ghost town(Central City–Black Hawk Historic District | Colorado encyclopedia).</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</em></p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=630
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"$250,000 Cost of Re-Opening Opera House\"</strong></br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192</br>\nJuly 10, 1932</p>\n\n<p>$250,000 is over 10 times the amount spent to construct the building in 1878 (25 Things You Didn’t Know About Central City Opera, 2020), and would cost almost 6 million dollars today. Delos and Enda Chappell financed much of the restoration, and likely did not make their money back, with 7 performances and a ticket price of $2.75. The large investments put into the theater show the belief in success for the opera house’s future and the revival of the surrounding town.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</p></em>"
  AFTER:  "<p><strong>\"$250,000 Cost of Re-Opening Opera House\"</strong><br>\nThe Rocky Mountain News, Volume 73, Number 192<br>\nJuly 10, 1932</p>\n\n<p>$250,000 is over 10 times the amount spent to construct the building in 1878 (25 Things You Didn’t Know About Central City Opera, 2020), and would cost almost 6 million dollars today. --- tbl_media_library.description id=632
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"Central City Opens Bonanza Theater\"</strong></br>\nThe New York Times, Volume 81, Number 27, 203</br>\nJuly 16, 1932</p>\n\n<p>“Men, women and children from the Atlantic Seaboard and the Pacific Coast” sat in the 1878 original chairs, now engraved with dedications to the pioneers, paid for by their descendants to fund the restoration, including dedications to John Evans, William Gilpin, and John Gregory (<em>The Glory that was Gold</em>, 1932). There, in 1932, the past collided with the present, and the nation with Central City. With renewed attention, the once small mining town began to boom again.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy The New York Times</em></p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=634
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>\"Woke Sleeping Theater</strong><br>\nAlberta Pike, The Rocky Mountain News, Volume 74, Number 21<br>\nAugust 6, 1933</strong></p>\n\n<p>“Wouldn’t it be marvelous if that lovely old theater, which had known such a fine tradition, could be made to live again!” said Ida Kruse McFarlane, who grew up in Central City. She emphasizes community support behind the reopening effort, with the opera originally providing happiness during economic prosperity, and later helping to revive the town economically. A year after reopening, many were already calling it an “American Salzburg,” the predominant summer opera in Europe.</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy Colorado Historic Newspaper Collection</em></p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=641
  BEFORE: "<p><strong>With a Bow to Miners</strong><br>\nJay Peake, The New York Times, Volume 119, Number 41,049<br>\nJune 14, 1970</p>\n\n<p>The annual traditions still drew crowds 40 years later. After the daily festivities ended, “not everyone stayed for the opera.” The festival had become about more than opera; it had become a celebration of the history of Central City and the revival effort. Central City became recognized as a National Historic Landmark in 1961 (Central City–Black Hawk Historic District | Colorado encyclopedia), and the opera house as a one in its own right, a few years after this article was written, in 1973 (Leyendecker & Eberhart, 1971).</p>\n\n<p><em>Courtesy The New York Times</em></p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=733
  BEFORE: "\"[Cooking with my dad] was his way of teaching me. He is very closed person, very strict. It’s hard because he is never going to tell me, 'I love you, son.' Teaching me to cook was his way of showing me what he knew how to do, try to pass it on to me. At first, it was fun, learning, coming from school Friday, Saturday night; going to work; making a little bit of money. Along the way, he taught me a lot, but sometimes, he was really tough on me. Sometimes, I had to quit my soccer tournaments because he wanted me at the restaurant. At the time, as a teenager, I saw it as hard and personal. But now that I have my own kids, I’m grateful for it. He showed me how to work and be disciplined.\"</p>"
  AFTER:  "\"[Cooking with my dad] was his way of teaching me. He is very closed person, very strict. It’s hard because he is never going to tell me, 'I love you, son.' At first, it was fun, learning, coming from school Friday, Saturday night; going to work; making a little bit of money. Along the way, he taught me a lot, but sometimes, he was really tough on me. Sometimes, I had to quit my soccer tournaments because he wanted me at the restaurant. At the time, as a teenager, I saw it as hard and personal. But now that I have my own kids, I’m grateful for it. He showed me how to work and be disciplined.\"<p></p>"

--- tbl_media_library.name id=748
  BEFORE: "<strong>Milagros Vega Valverde</strong><br>\n<em>(Lima, Peru)</em>"
  AFTER:  "Milagros Vega Valverde\n(Lima, Peru)"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=748
  BEFORE: "“Conozco a una señora y le cuento mi situación. Me dice: ‘Sabes qué, Erika, aquí hay una iglesia, hay una institución donde dan comida gratis.’ Me llevó, me inscribió, me dio la mano. Con la comida que ellos me habían regalado, yo de agradecimiento, preparé un buffet en mi casa y lo llevé. Les encantó. Me dijeron: ‘Sabes qué, vamos a hacer otro evento y te vamos a contratar para que puedas vender tu comida y todos puedan conocer tu comida.’ Me motiva el querer hacer conocer mis raíces. Estaba un poco preocupada porque no sabía dónde encontrar los condimentos y los productos peruanos. Hasta que en los mercados mexicanos encontré algunos productos. Entonces dije: ‘No. Yo tengo que cocinar mi comida y que la gente la conozca.’ El desafío me motivó a que las personas prueben mi comida con mi cultura. También empecé a fusionar con la comida mexicana, con la americana. Y es lo que me motiva, que la gente sepa que se pueden hacer muchas cosas con la comida, con mis raíces, con mis condimentos y con los productos peruanos que están aquí en Estados Unidos.” English Translation: I met a woman and told her about my situation. She said, “You know what, Erika, there’s a church here, an organization where they give out free food.” She took me there, registered me, and gave me a hand. With the food they had given me, out of gratitude I prepared a buffet at home and brought it over. They loved it. They said, “You know what, we’re going to do another event and we’re going to hire you so you can sell your food and everyone can get to know your food.”\nI’m motivated by wanting to make my roots known. I was a little worried because I didn’t know where to find Peruvian seasonings and products—until I found some items at Mexican markets. Then I said, “No. I have to cook my food so people can get to know it.” The challenge motivated me to have people try my food with my culture. I also started fusing it with Mexican food and with American food. And that’s what motivates me—that people know there’s so much you can do with food, with my roots, with my seasonings, and with the Peruvian products that are here in the United States."
  AFTER:  "<p>“Conozco a una señora y le cuento mi situación. Me dice: ‘Sabes qué, Erika, aquí hay una iglesia, hay una institución donde dan comida gratis.’ Me llevó, me inscribió, me dio la mano. Con la comida que ellos me habían regalado, yo de agradecimiento, preparé un buffet en mi casa y lo llevé. Les encantó. Me dijeron: ‘Sabes qué, vamos a hacer otro evento y te vamos a contratar para que puedas vender tu comida y todos puedan conocer tu comida.’ Me motiva el querer hacer conocer mis raíces. Estaba un poco preocupada porque no sabía dónde encontrar los condimentos y los productos peruanos. Hasta que en los mercados mexicanos encontré algunos productos. Entonces dije: ‘No. Yo tengo que cocinar mi comida y que la gente la conozca.’ El desafío me motivó a que las personas prueben mi comida con mi cultura. También empecé a fusionar con la comida mexicana, con la americana. Y es lo que me motiva, que la gente sepa que se pueden hacer muchas cosas con la comida, con mis raíces, con mis condimentos y con los productos peruanos que están aquí en Estados Unidos.” English Translation: I met a woman and told her about my situation. She said, “You know what, Erika, there’s a church here, an organization where they give out free food.” She took me there, registered me, and gave me a hand. With the food they had given me, out of gratitude I prepared a buffet at home and brought it over. They loved it. They said, “You know what, we’re going to do another event and we’re going to hire you so you can sell your food and everyone can get to know your food.”<br>I’m motivated by wanting to make my roots known. I was a little worried because I didn’t know where to find Peruvian seasonings and products—until I found some items at Mexican markets. Then I said, “No. I have to cook my food so people can get to know it.” The challenge motivated me to have people try my food with my culture. I also started fusing it with Mexican food and with American food. And that’s what motivates me—that people know there’s so much you can do with food, with my roots, with my seasonings, and with the Peruvian products that are here in the United States.</p>"
--- tbl_media_library.name id=749
  BEFORE: "<strong>Manny Barella</strong><br>\n<em>(Monterrey, Mexico)</em>"
  AFTER:  "Manny Barella\n(Monterrey, Mexico)"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=749
  BEFORE: "“Quiero que la gente pruebe y decir: ‘Ah, me recuerda a cuando estaba en Monterrey y comía eso.’  Tengo un postre, el Carlota de limón, por ejemplo. Era el postre que cuando estaba en la prepa y hacíamos fiestas, había como cinco versiones del mismo postre. Como los brownies, haz de cuenta. Todo el mundo hacía brownies. Todo el mundo hacía Carlota. Y me ha pasado, cuando tenía mi restaurante de cocina mexicana, hace varios años, gente de Monterrey que iba porque escuchaban que el chef era de Monterrey. Y aprovechaban, ‘pues, déjame ir a ver.’ Probaban la barbacoa, probaban el carlota, y decían: ‘Es que transporté a cuando estaba en Monterrey.’ Y para mí, esa es la frase que estoy buscando: que la gente diga ‘me transportaste a México.’ No quiere decir que todos los platillos van a ser exitosos desde esa perspectiva, pero esa es la meta. Que alguien pueda probar algo y decir: ‘Ah, sí, sabe como cuando estuve en Guanajuato y probé esto,’ o ‘sabe como cuando estuve en Oaxaca y probé un mole…’ Eso, para mí, es la meta: que la gente viaje a través de mi comida de la misma manera que yo he viajado a través de la comida.” English Translation: I want people to taste it and say, “Ah, this reminds me of when I was in Monterrey and ate this.” I have a dessert—Carlota de limón, for example. Back in high school, whenever we had parties, there were like five versions of the same dessert. Like brownies, you know. Everyone made brownies. Everyone made Carlota.\nAnd it’s happened that, when I had my Mexican restaurant years ago, people from Monterrey would come because they’d heard the chef was from Monterrey. They’d think, “Well, let me go check it out.” They’d try the barbacoa, they’d try the carlota, and they’d say, “It transported me back to when I was in Monterrey.” And for me, that’s the phrase I’m looking for: for people to say, “You transported me to Mexico.” It doesn’t mean every dish will be successful from that perspective, but that’s the goal: that someone can taste something and say, “Ah, yes, it tastes like when I was in Guanajuato and I tried this,” or “it tastes like when I was in Oaxaca and I tried a mole…” That, for me, is the goal: that people travel through my food in the same way I have traveled through food."
  AFTER:  "<p>“Quiero que la gente pruebe y decir: ‘Ah, me recuerda a cuando estaba en Monterrey y comía eso.’  Tengo un postre, el Carlota de limón, por ejemplo. Era el postre que cuando estaba en la prepa y hacíamos fiestas, había como cinco versiones del mismo postre. Como los brownies, haz de cuenta. Todo el mundo hacía brownies. Todo el mundo hacía Carlota. Y me ha pasado, cuando tenía mi restaurante de cocina mexicana, hace varios años, gente de Monterrey que iba porque escuchaban que el chef era de Monterrey. Y aprovechaban, ‘pues, déjame ir a ver.’ Probaban la barbacoa, probaban el carlota, y decían: ‘Es que transporté a cuando estaba en Monterrey.’ Y para mí, esa es la frase que estoy buscando: que la gente diga ‘me transportaste a México.’ No quiere decir que todos los platillos van a ser exitosos desde esa perspectiva, pero esa es la meta. Que alguien pueda probar algo y decir: ‘Ah, sí, sabe como cuando estuve en Guanajuato y probé esto,’ o ‘sabe como cuando estuve en Oaxaca y probé un mole…’ Eso, para mí, es la meta: que la gente viaje a través de mi comida de la misma manera que yo he viajado a través de la comida.” English Translation: I want people to taste it and say, “Ah, this reminds me of when I was in Monterrey and ate this.” I have a dessert—Carlota de limón, for example. Back in high school, whenever we had parties, there were like five versions of the same dessert. Like brownies, you know. Everyone made brownies. Everyone made Carlota.<br>And it’s happened that, when I had my Mexican restaurant years ago, people from Monterrey would come because they’d heard the chef was from Monterrey. They’d think, “Well, let me go check it out.” They’d try the barbacoa, they’d try the carlota, and they’d say, “It transported me back to when I was in Monterrey.” And for me, that’s the phrase I’m looking for: for people to say, “You transported me to Mexico.” It doesn’t mean every dish will be successful from that perspective, but that’s the goal: that someone can taste something and say, “Ah, yes, it tastes like when I was in Guanajuato and I tried this,” or “it tastes like when I was in Oaxaca and I tried a mole…” That, for me, is the goal: that people travel through my food in the same way I have traveled through food.</p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1016
  BEFORE: "DU Student Yearbook, Kynewisbok, Vol. 54, pg. 101\n1951 – 1952\n\nIn the 1940s, waxing and ski maintenance were largely informal and skier-led, shaped by individual experience and local conditions. By the 1950s and 1960s, programs such as DU’s ski team increasingly treated equipment setup, including waxing choices, binding adjustment, and ski inspection, as a collective, coach-directed component of training. These practices responded to more regular competitive schedules, changing ski technology, and the influence of European alpine coaching systems."
  AFTER:  "<p>DU Student Yearbook, Kynewisbok, Vol. 54, pg. 101<br>1951 – 1952</p><p>In the 1940s, waxing and ski maintenance were largely informal and skier-led, shaped by individual experience and local conditions. By the 1950s and 1960s, programs such as DU’s ski team increasingly treated equipment setup, including waxing choices, binding adjustment, and ski inspection, as a collective, coach-directed component of training. These practices responded to more regular competitive schedules, changing ski technology, and the influence of European alpine coaching systems.</p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1030
  BEFORE: "DU Student Yearbook, Kynewisbok, Vol. 47, pg. 129\n1944-1945\n\nThe photograph shows skiers climbing a mountain slope in Colorado at a time when uphill travel remained a regular part of skiing. Before chairlifts and surface lifts became common, skiers relied on their own power to reach the top of a run, which limited the number of descents and shaped how skiing was practiced. The image documents this approach just before postwar lift development expanded access and changed how people moved across Colorado’s mountain terrain."
  AFTER:  "<p>DU Student Yearbook, Kynewisbok, Vol. 47, pg. 129<br>1944-1945</p><p>The photograph shows skiers climbing a mountain slope in Colorado at a time when uphill travel remained a regular part of skiing. Before chairlifts and surface lifts became common, skiers relied on their own power to reach the top of a run, which limited the number of descents and shaped how skiing was practiced. The image documents this approach just before postwar lift development expanded access and changed how people moved across Colorado’s mountain terrain.</p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1095
  BEFORE: "Luis “Junior” Martinez was the founder of Baile de Chicano de Aztlan and the local chapter of the Black Berets. He was killed outside of an apartment building owned by the Crusade for Justice by a Denver Police officer. Crusade for Justice was a Chicano and Chicana social justice organization that was known to be militant.\nImage courtesy of History Colorado and Robert Gonzalez."

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1096
  BEFORE: "March 17: The police raid began with the death of Luis “Junior” Martinez and ended with an explosion. There were disagreements about the origins of the explosion. In the aftermath, graffiti was written on the ruined wall that read: \"We are not beaten and we do not intend be beaten or [illegible] such... what has happened here is but the sound before the fury of those who are oppressed.\"\nImage courtesy of History Colorado and Juan Espinosa."
  AFTER:  "<p>March 17: The police raid began with the death of Luis “Junior” Martinez and ended with an explosion. There were disagreements about the origins of the explosion. In the aftermath, graffiti was written on the ruined wall that read: \"We are not beaten and we do not intend be beaten or [illegible] such... what has happened here is but the sound before the fury of those who are oppressed.\"<br>Image courtesy of History Colorado and Juan Espinosa.</p>"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=1097
  BEFORE: "Clyde Bellecourt, co-founder and National Field Director of the American Indian Movement (AIM), held a rally in Denver. His goal was to generate support for the Wounded Knee Occupation that was happening on the Pine Ridge Reservation in South Dakota. On December 29, 1890, more than 150 Lakota Sioux people, including women and children, were killed by a U.S. Army's 7th Cavalry in the Wounded Knee Massacre. Then, in 1973, members of AIM occupied the site of Wounded Knee for 71 days to protest conditions on the Pine Ridge reservation.\nImage courtesy of the Denver Post."
  AFTER:  "<p>Clyde Bellecourt, co-founder and National Field Director of the American Indian Movement (AIM), held a rally in Denver. His goal was to generate support for the Wounded Knee Occupation that was happening on the Pine Ridge Reservation in South Dakota. On December 29, 1890, more than 150 Lakota Sioux people, including women and children, were killed by a U.S. Army's 7th Cavalry in the Wounded Knee Massacre. Then, in 1973, members of AIM occupied the site of Wounded Knee for 71 days to protest conditions on the Pine Ridge reservation.<br>Image courtesy of the Denver Post.</p>"

  BEFORE: "April 28: Clyde Bellecourt, co-founder of the American Indian Movement, Angela Davis, founding member of the Committees of Correspondence for Democracy and Socialism and professor at the University of California, Santa Cruz, and Rodolfo \"Corky\" Gonzales, leader of the Crusade for Justice, spoke at a rally of the Denver Chicano Liberation Defense Committee to promote unity among historically marginalized and minority Americans.\nImage courtesy of Dave Buresh and Denver Post."
  AFTER:  "<p>April 28: Clyde Bellecourt, co-founder of the American Indian Movement, Angela Davis, founding member of the Committees of Correspondence for Democracy and Socialism and professor at the University of California, Santa Cruz, and Rodolfo \"Corky\" Gonzales, leader of the Crusade for Justice, spoke at a rally of the Denver Chicano Liberation Defense Committee to promote unity among historically marginalized and minority Americans.<br>Image courtesy of Dave Buresh and Denver Post.</p>"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=1099
  BEFORE: "June: Naomi Lipsitz, Rhoda Friedman, Sue Gottsegen, Lil Hoffman, and Roz Duman,, five Denver women, attended the Freedom Assembly outside the U.S. Capitol Building in Washington to protest the treatment of Soviet Jewish citizens. The Soviet Jewry Movement (1963-1990) advocated for the rights of Jewish people to emigrate out of Russia and to practice their religion without state persecution or discrimination.\nImage courtesy of Beck Archives, University Libraries."

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1103
  BEFORE: "May: Sakura Square was established in May 1973 at 20th Street between Lawrence and Larimer Streets. The mission of Sakura Square is to retain the multigenerational and multi-cultural community and serve community members seeking Japanese goods and services. Since the 1900s, the neighborhood was known as \"Japantown.\" However, in the early 1970s Denver's Urban Renewal Authority began a renewal project that demolished thirty blocks of \"decaying downtown.\" The area is now known as LoDo.\nImage courtesy of the Sakura Foundation."
  AFTER:  "<p>May: Sakura Square was established in May 1973 at 20th Street between Lawrence and Larimer Streets. The mission of Sakura Square is to retain the multigenerational and multi-cultural community and serve community members seeking Japanese goods and services. Since the 1900s, the neighborhood was known as \"Japantown.\" However, in the early 1970s Denver's Urban Renewal Authority began a renewal project that demolished thirty blocks of \"decaying downtown.\" The area is now known as LoDo.<br>Image courtesy of the Sakura Foundation.</p>"
--- tbl_media_library.description id=1104
  BEFORE: "October 14: Nearly 800 people rallied together to raise $15,000 to support the welfare of Jewish people living in Israel and freeing of Jewish people in the Soviet Union. They marched five miles from Bible Park to the Jewish Community Center and then took part in the “Simchas Torah” freedom festival organized by the Anti-Defamation League of the Denver B’nai B’rith.\nImage courtesy of Rocky Mountain News, and Beck Archives, University Libraries."
  AFTER:  "<p>October 14: Nearly 800 people rallied together to raise $15,000 to support the welfare of Jewish people living in Israel and freeing of Jewish people in the Soviet Union. --- tbl_media_library.description id=1121
  BEFORE: "The national Civil Rights Commission members spoke to campus in various locations, including to the Black Experience in America seminar, in May. The members consisted of Frankie Muse Freeman, an attorney from St. Louis, MO, DU Chancellor Maurice Mitchell, and Joe Muskrat, former assistant attorney general of Oklahoma. They spoke critically of the federal government’s administration policies, especially recent funding policies.\nImage courtesy of DU Clarion and DU Special Collections & Archives."
  AFTER:  "<p>The national Civil Rights Commission members spoke to campus in various locations, including to the Black Experience in America seminar, in May. The members consisted of Frankie Muse Freeman, an attorney from St. Louis, MO, DU Chancellor Maurice Mitchell, and Joe Muskrat, former assistant attorney general of Oklahoma. They spoke critically of the federal government’s administration policies, especially recent funding policies.<br>Image courtesy of DU Clarion and DU Special Collections &amp; Archives.</p>"

  BEFORE: "Representatives of MUW and WUW\nRepresentatives of Mweusi Wazuri (MUW) and Wanawake Uso Wazuri (WUW) student organizations accuse the student senate of misallocating funds and support in favor of mainly white organizations on May 21st.\nImage courtesy of DU Clarion and DU Special Collections & Archives."

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1123
  BEFORE: "John W. Rice\nAssociate Dean of the College of Arts and Sciences Rice was promoted to Assistant Vice Chancellor for Student Affairs. He was promoted to assist in righting the disadvantages students of color faced in admissions and the allocation of resources. He taught the Black Experience in America seminar. \nImage courtesy of DU Clarion and DU Special Collections & Archives."
  AFTER:  "<p>John W. Rice<br>Associate Dean of the College of Arts and Sciences Rice was promoted to Assistant Vice Chancellor for Student Affairs. He was promoted to assist in righting the disadvantages students of color faced in admissions and the allocation of resources. He taught the Black Experience in America seminar. <br>Image courtesy of DU Clarion and DU Special Collections &amp; Archives.</p>"

--- tbl_media_library.description id=1130
  BEFORE: "Vice Chancellor of Student Affairs John W. Rice lectured to the Black Experience in America seminar on October 3rd about Black history. He emphasized that “Every time there is an attempt at self-determination, we are cut off.”\nImage courtesy of DU Special Collections & Archives."
  AFTER:  "<p>Vice Chancellor of Student Affairs John W. Rice lectured to the Black Experience in America seminar on October 3rd about Black history. He emphasized that “Every time there is an attempt at self-determination, we are cut off.”<br>Image courtesy of DU Special Collections &amp; Archives.</p>"
